Traveling wave solutions to the inclined or periodic free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes equations Thanks: I. Tice was supported by an NSF CAREER Grant (DMS #1653161).
Junichi Koganemaru, Ian Tice

Abstract
. This paper concerns the construction of traveling wave solutions to the free boundary incompressible Navier-Stokes system. We study a single layer of viscous fluid in a strip-like domain that is bounded below by a flat rigid surface and above by a moving surface. The fluid is acted upon by a bulk force and a surface stress that are stationary in a coordinate system moving parallel to the fluid bottom. We also assume that the fluid is subject to a uniform gravitational force that can be resolved into a sum of a vertical component and a component lying in the direction of the traveling wave velocity. This configuration arises, for instance, in the modeling of fluid flow down an inclined plane. We also study the effect of periodicity by allowing the fluid cross section to be periodic in various directions. The horizontal component of the gravitational field gives rise to stationary solutions that are pure shear flows, and we construct our solutions as perturbations of these by means of an implicit function argument. An essential component of our analysis is the development of some new functional analytic properties of a scale of anisotropic Sobolev spaces, including that these spaces are an algebra in the supercritical regime, which may be of independent interest.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q30, 35R35, 35C07; Secondary 46J10, 76D45, 76E05

1.
Introduction

The existence of traveling wave solutions to the equations of fluid mechanics has been a subject of intense study for nearly two centuries (see Section
1.4
for a brief summary). Until recently, most of the mathematical results in this area focused on inviscid fluids, but work in the last few years constructed traveling wave solutions to the free boundary Navier-Stokes equations with a single horizontally infinite but finite depth layer of incompressible fluid
[
18
]
, and with multiple layers
[
25
]
in a uniform, downward-pointing gravitational field. The purpose of the present paper is to extend these constructions into more general physical configurations by considering two effects: inclination of the fluid domain, which results in a component of the gravitational field parallel to the fluid layer; and, periodicity of the fluid layer in certain directions. The key to the constructions in
[
18
,
25
]
was the identification and utilization of a new scale of anisotropic Sobolev spaces, which serve as the container space for the function describing the free surface of the fluid. The results in this paper rely crucially on some new functional analytic properties of these spaces, which we prove here: the development of this scale of spaces on domains with periodicity; and, the fact that these spaces form an algebra in the supercritical regime. While these results are essential for our specific PDE needs, they may be of independent interest to those interested in Sobolev spaces.

1.1.
Kinematic and dynamic description of the fluid

In this paper we consider a single finite depth layer of viscous, incompressible fluid. We assume that the fluid evolves in a strip-like domain, bounded below by a flat, rigid, inclined surface and above by a free moving surface that can be described by the graph of a continuous function, in dimensions
n\geqslant 2
(though, of course, only the cases
n\in\{2,3\}
are physically relevant). Furthermore, we assume that the fluid is subject to a uniform gravitational field
\mathfrak{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Prior to specifying the fluid domain or the equations of motion, we fix an orthogonal coordinate system as follows. We choose the unit vector
e_{n}
to be normal to the inclined surface, and we choose the unit vector
e_{1}
in such a way that
\mathfrak{G}
lies in the
e_{1}
-
e_{n}
hyperplane. In other words, we posit that the uniform gravitational field
\mathfrak{G}
resolves into two components as
\mathfrak{G}=\kappa e_{1}-\mathfrak{g}e_{n}
, where
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathfrak{g}\in(0,\infty)
. We can then define the angle of incline of the domain,
\theta\in(-\pi/2,\pi/2)
, via
\tan\theta=\kappa/\mathfrak{g}
(see Figure
1
).

Next we turn to a description of the fluid cross-section,
\Sigma
, which will allow us to specify the free surface and rigid bottom of the fluid and to model periodicity. The flat rigid bottom of the fluid, which is orthogonal to the
e_{n}
direction, is described by
(n-1)
-dimensional sets of the form

\displaystyle\Sigma=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}\times\prod_{i=2}^{n-1}\Sigma_{i},&\text{where }\Sigma_{i}=\mathbb{R}\text{ or else }\Sigma_{i}=L_{i}\mathbb{T}\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}L_{i}\mathbb{T},\end{cases}

(1.1)

where
L\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z}
denotes the 1-torus of periodicity length
L>0
. This choice of
\Sigma
allows us to model: full periodicity in all directions, which corresponds to the second case; no periodicity, which corresponds to the first case with each
\Sigma_{i}=\mathbb{R}
; or partial periodicity when
n\geqslant 3
, which corresponds to the first case with at least one
\Sigma_{i}=L_{i}\mathbb{T}
. However, for technical reasons that we will detail below, in the case of partial periodicity we cannot allow periodicity in the
e_{1}
direction, and so the first factor of
\Sigma
must be
\mathbb{R}
. To be more explicit in the physically relevant cases
n\in\{2,3\}
, we note that (
1.1
) allows for
\Sigma\in\{\mathbb{R},L\mathbb{T}\}
when
n=2
, and when
n=3
it allows for
\Sigma\in\{\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R}\times L_{2}\mathbb{T},L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times L_{2}\mathbb{T}\}
, but we exclude the possibility that
\Sigma=L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. In any dimension, we endow
\Sigma
with the usual topological and smooth structure.

With
\Sigma
in hand, we can now describe strip-like
n
-dimensional domains with cross section
\Sigma
. Given any function
\upzeta:\Sigma\to(0,\infty)
, we define the set
\Omega_{\upzeta}\subset\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}
via
\Omega_{\upzeta}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\mid 0<x_{n}<\upzeta(x^{\prime})\}

and the
\upzeta
-graph surface
\Sigma_{\upzeta}\subset\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}
via
\Sigma_{\upzeta}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\mid x_{n}=\upzeta(x^{\prime})\text{ for some }x^{\prime}\in\Sigma\}
. We note that if
\upzeta
is continuous, then the upper boundary of
\Omega_{\upzeta}
is
\Sigma_{\upzeta}
and its lower boundary is given by
\Sigma_{0}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\mid x_{n}=0\}.

We assume that at equilibrium, with all external forces and stresses absent, the fluid occupies the flat equilibrium domain
\Omega_{b}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\mid 0<x_{n}<b\}

for some equilibrium depth parameter
b>0
. Furthermore, we assume that under perturbation, the fluid occupies the time-dependent fluid domain
\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}
, where
\zeta:\Sigma\times[0,\infty)\to(-b,\infty)
is an unknown free surface function. The fluid is then bounded above by the
(b+\zeta)
-graph surface
\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}
and below by the flat boundary
\Sigma_{0}
.

Figure 1.
A sample portion of the time-dependent fluid domain under perturbation

In addition to the aforementioned gravitational force, we posit that there are four other distinct forces acting upon the fluid: one in the bulk and three on the free surface. The first bulk force is a generic force described by the vector field
\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}(\cdot,t):\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The first surface force is a constant external pressure
P_{ext}\in\mathbb{R}
applied by the fluid above the free surface. The second surface force is generated by an externally applied stress tensor, which is described by a map
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}:\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym}
, where
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym}=\{M\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\mid M=M^{T}\}

is the set of symmetric
n\times n
matrices. The symmetry condition is imposed to be consistent with the fact that stress tensors are typically symmetric in continuum mechanics, but this condition is not essential and could be dropped in our analysis. The third surface force is the surface tension generated by the surface itself, which we model in the standard way as
-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta)
, where
\sigma\geqslant 0
is the coefficient of the surface tension, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\zeta)=\diverge^{\prime}\left(\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\zeta}{\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\prime}\zeta|^{2}}}\right)

(1.2)

is the mean-curvature operator.

We assume that the evolution of the fluid is described for time
t\geqslant 0
by its velocity field
w(\cdot,t):\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and its pressure
P(\cdot,t):\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\to\mathbb{R}
. For each
t>0
, we require that the fluid velocity
w
, the pressure
P
, and the free surface
\zeta
satisfy the free boundary incompressible Navier Stokes equations:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\rho(\partial_{t}w+w\cdot\nabla w)-\mu\Delta w+\nabla P=-\rho\mathfrak{g}e_{n}+\rho\kappa e_{1}+\tilde{\mathfrak{f}},&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
\diverge w=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
\partial_{t}\zeta=w\cdot\nu\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\prime}\zeta|^{2}},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
(PI-\mu\mathbb{D}w)\nu=[-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta)I+P_{ext}I+\tilde{\mathcal{T}}]\nu,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
w=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.3)

Here
\rho>0
is the fluid density,
\mu>0
is the fluid viscosity,
\mathbb{D}w=(\nabla w)+(\nabla w)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{sym}
is the symmetrized gradient of
w
, and
\nu(\cdot,t)=(-\nabla^{\prime}\zeta(\cdot,t),1)/\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\prime}\zeta(\cdot,t)|^{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

is the outward pointing unit normal to the surface
\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}
. The first two equations in (
1.3
) are the standard incompressible Navier-Stokes equations; the first equation asserts the Newtonian balance of forces, the second enforces the conservation of mass. The third equation is the kinematic boundary condition describing the evolution of the free surface with the fluid. We note in particular that the third equation may be written as a transport equation in the form of
\partial_{t}\zeta+\nabla^{\prime}\zeta\cdot w^{\prime}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}}=w_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}},
which shows that the free surface
\zeta
is transported by the horizontal component of the velocity
w^{\prime}
and driven by the vertical component of the velocity
w_{n}
. The fourth equation encodes the dynamic boundary conditions asserting the balance of forces on the free surface. The fifth equation is the typical no-slip condition enforced on flat rigid surfaces.

For the sake of convenience, we shall assume without loss of generality that
\rho=\mu=\mathfrak{g}=1
. This can be achieved by dividing both sides of the first equation in (
1.3
) by
\rho
, rescaling the space-time variables, and renaming
b,\sigma,\kappa
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}
,
P_{ext}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
, and (in the periodic settings) the periodicity length scales
L_{i}
, accordingly. This yields the system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}w+w\cdot\nabla w-\Delta w+\nabla P=-e_{n}+\kappa e_{1}+\tilde{\mathfrak{f}},&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
\diverge w=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
\partial_{t}\zeta=w\cdot\nu\sqrt{1+|\nabla^{\prime}\zeta|^{2}},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
(PI-\mathbb{D}w)\nu=[-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta)I+P_{ext}I+\tilde{\mathcal{T}}]\nu,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
w=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.4)

For a differentiable vector field
u
and a scalar
p
, we define the stress tensor
S(p,u)=pI-\mathbb{D}u\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym}
, where
I
is the
n\times n
identity and
\mathbb{D}u
is the symmetrized gradient of
u
. By defining the divergence operator to act on tensors in the canonical way, we have
\diverge S(p,u)=\nabla p-\Delta u+\nabla\diverge u
. This means that in the first equation of (
1.4
) we can rewrite

\nabla P-\Delta w=\diverge S(P,w).

(1.5)

1.2.
Shear flows and perturbations

The system (
1.4
) admits steady shear flow solutions that reduce to hydrostatic equilibrium when
\kappa=0
. To see this, we suppose that
\zeta=0,\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}=0,\tilde{\mathcal{T}}=0
. We then define the smooth functions
s_{0},s:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle s_{0}(x_{n})=bx_{n}-\frac{x_{n}^{2}}{2}\text{ and }s(x_{n})=\kappa s_{0}(x_{n}).

(1.6)

We then define the steady shear velocity field
U^{1}:\Omega_{b}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
via

\displaystyle W^{1}(x^{\prime},x_{n})=s(x_{n})e_{1},

(1.7)

and the equilibrium hydrostatic pressure
p:\Omega_{b}\to\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle p(x^{\prime},x_{n})=P_{ext}-x_{n}+b.

(1.8)

See Figure
1
for a sketch of the
W^{1}
profile. One can readily check that
w=W^{1},P=p,\zeta=0
is a steady shear flow solution to (
1.4
) when
\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}=0,\tilde{\mathcal{T}}=0
. Note that these shear flow solutions are special solutions induced by
\kappa\neq 0
, and they exist due to the presence of viscosity, with no clear analogue in the Euler system. In the literature, these solutions are sometimes referred to as Nusselt solutions.

We will study the system (
1.4
) as a perturbation of this steady solution. We define the perturbation of the velocity field and pressure field given by

\displaystyle\overline{w}^{1}(x,t)=w(x,t)+W^{1}(x),\quad\overline{P}^{1}(x,t)=P(x,t)+p(x),

(1.9)

and we see that
(\overline{w}^{1},\overline{P}^{1},\zeta)
is a solution to (
1.4
) if and only if
(w,P,\zeta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{t}w+\diverge S(P,w)+(w+W^{1})\cdot\nabla(w+W^{1})=\tilde{\mathfrak{f}},&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
\diverge w=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
\partial_{t}\zeta+\nabla^{\prime}\zeta\cdot w^{\prime}+(\partial_{1}\zeta)s(\zeta+b)=w_{n},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
S(P,w)\nu=[(\zeta-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta))I+\mathcal{\tilde{T}}-\kappa\zeta(e_{1}\otimes e_{n}+e_{n}\otimes e_{1})]\nu,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\zeta(\cdot,t)}\\
w=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0},\end{cases}

(1.10)

Here we have utilized the identity (
1.5
) in the first equation of (
1.10
), and the tensor product
v\otimes w\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
of two vectors
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined in the standard way via
(v\otimes w)_{ij}=v_{i}w_{j}
.

1.3.
Traveling wave solutions around shear flows

In this paper our main goal is to construct traveling wave solutions to (
1.10
), which are solutions that are stationary when viewed in a coordinate system moving at a constant speed. For this stationary condition to hold, the moving coordinate system must travel at a constant velocity parallel to the flat rigid surface
\Sigma_{0}
. In this paper we assume that the traveling waves move at a constant velocity in the direction of incline; in other words, that the moving coordinate system’s velocity relative to the Eulerian coordinates of (
1.3
) is
\gamma e_{1}
for some
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
. The speed of the traveling wave is then
|\gamma|
, and
\sgn(\gamma)
indicates the direction of travel along the
e_{1}
axis.

Next we proceed to reformulate the problem in the traveling coordinates. We assume that the stationary free surface is given by an unknown function
\eta:\Sigma\to(-b,\infty)
, and it is related to
\zeta
via
\zeta(x^{\prime},t)=\eta(x^{\prime}-\gamma te_{1})
. The stationary velocity, pressure, force, and stress
v,q,\mathfrak{f},\mathcal{T}
are related to
w,P,\tilde{\mathfrak{f}},\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
via

\displaystyle w(x,t)=v(x-\gamma te_{1}),\quad P(x,t)=q(x-\gamma te_{1}),\quad\tilde{\mathfrak{f}}(x,t)=\mathfrak{f}(x-\gamma te_{1}),\quad\tilde{\mathcal{T}}(x,t)=\mathcal{T}(x^{\prime}-\gamma te_{1}).

(1.11)

Then using (
1.11
), the system (
1.10
) reduces to a time-independent system for
(v,q,\eta)
given the forcing terms
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(q,v)-\gamma e_{1}\cdot\nabla v+(v+W^{1})\cdot\nabla(v+W^{1})=\mathfrak{f},&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\eta}\\
\diverge v=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\eta}\\
-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta+\nabla^{\prime}\eta\cdot v^{\prime}+(\partial_{1}\eta)s(\eta+b)=v_{n},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\eta}\\
S(q,v)\mathcal{N}=[(\eta-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\eta))I+\mathcal{T}-\kappa\eta(e_{1}\otimes e_{n}+e_{n}\otimes e_{1})]\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\eta}\\
v=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0},\end{cases}

(1.12)

where we have defined the non-unit normal vector field

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=(-\nabla^{\prime}\eta,1).

(1.13)

For technical reasons to be discussed later in Section
1.7
, it is convenient for us to remove the
\eta
terms appearing in the fourth equation of (
1.12
) by introducing an additional time-independent perturbation term

\displaystyle\quad W^{2}(x^{\prime},x_{n})=\kappa x_{n}\eta(x^{\prime})e_{1}

(1.14)

and by defining the modified shear velocity and modified pressure via

\displaystyle\overline{v}^{1}(x,t)=v+W^{2}(x),\;\overline{q}^{1}(x)=q(x)+\eta(x^{\prime}).

(1.15)

One may readily check that
(\overline{v}^{1},\overline{q}^{1},\zeta)
is a solution to (
1.12
) if and only if
(v,q,\eta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(q,v)-\gamma e_{1}\cdot\nabla v-\gamma\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}+(v+W^{1}+W^{2})\cdot\nabla(v+W^{1}+W^{2})+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)\\
\hskip 21.68121pt-\kappa x_{n}\Delta^{\prime}\eta(x^{\prime})e_{1}-\kappa(x_{n}\nabla^{\prime}\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta)=\mathfrak{f},&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\eta}\\
\diverge{v}+\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta(x^{\prime})=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\eta}\\
(-\gamma+s(\eta+b)+\kappa(\eta+b)\eta)\partial_{1}\eta=v\cdot\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\eta}\\
S(q,v)\mathcal{N}=[-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\eta)I+\mathcal{T}+\kappa(b+\eta)(e_{1}\otimes(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)\otimes e_{1})]\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\eta}\\
v=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.16)

We note that by using the perturbations introduced in (
1.15
), we have replaced the
\eta
terms in the fourth equation of (
1.12
) by either derivatives of
\eta
or products of
\eta
and its derivatives, at the price of introducing additional terms in the first equation.

1.4.
Previous work

The system (
1.3
) and its variants have been studied extensively in the mathematical literature. For a brief survey of the subject, we refer to Section 1.2 of Leoni-Tice
[
18
]
and Section 1.4 of Tice
[
28
]
. For a more thorough review of the subject, we refer the surveys of Tolland
[
29
]
, Groves
[
10
]
, Strauss
[
26
]
, the recent paper by Strauss et al.
[
15
]
in the inviscid case, and to the surveys of Zadrzyńska
[
32
]
and Shibata-Shimizu
[
23
]
in the viscous case.

When
\kappa=0
, the small data theory for the free boundary problem (
1.3
) over periodic domains is well-understood in dimension
n=3
. For the problem with surface tension, Nishida-Teramoto-Yoshihara
[
20
]
constructed global periodic solutions and proved that they decay exponentially fast to equilibrium. For the problem without surface tension, Hataya
[
14
]
constructed global solutions decaying at a fixed algebraic rate, and later Guo-Tice
[
12
]
constructed global solutions decaying almost exponentially. In the non-periodic setting, Beale
[
3
]
established the local-wellposedness of solutions without surface tension, and Beale
[
4
]
proved the global existence of solutions with surface tension. Beale-Nishida
[
5
]
later established that the aforementioned solutions decay at an algebraic rate. Tani-Tanaka
[
27
]
proved the global existence solutions with and without surface tension under milder assumptions on the initial data, but did not study their decay rates. Guo-Tice
[
13
]
proved that for the problem without surface tension, the global solutions decay at a fixed algebraic rate.

The investigation of the dynamics of viscous shear flows without free boundary is classical and dates back to the work of Orr
[
21
]
and Sommerfeld
[
24
]
, where they noted the so-called viscous destabilization phenomenon. This was subsequently investigated formally by many authors in the physics literature, including Heisenberg
[
16
]
, Lin
[
19
]
, and Tollmien
[
30
]
. However, it wasn’t until recently that a rigorous mathematical proof for the instability of viscous incompressible shear flows without free boundary appeared, in the work of Grenier-Guo-Nguyen
[
9
]
.

When
\kappa\neq 0
, much less is known about the dynamics of the free boundary problem (
1.3
). Ueno
[
31
]
studied the 2D problem with surface tension in the thin film regime and established uniform estimates of solutions with respect to the thinness parameter. Padula
[
22
]
studied the 3D problem with surface tension and proved sufficient conditions for asymptotic stability under a priori assumptions on the global existence of solutions. Tice
[
28
]
studied the asymptotic stability of shear flow solutions to the nonlinear problem with and without surface tension, and proved that solutions decay exponentially fast to equilibrium with surface tension and almost exponentially without surface tension.

The existence of traveling wave solutions without background shear flows to (
1.3
) first appeared in the recent work of Leoni-Tice
[
18
]
, and the results therein were extended by Stevenson-Tice
[
25
]
to a multilayer configuration. To the best of our knowledge, there are no known results on the existence of traveling wave solutions around shear flows in the case when
\kappa\neq 0
or when the cross section
\Sigma
is periodic.

1.5.
Reformulation in a fixed domain

We note that in (
1.16
), the fluid domain of interest
\Omega_{b+\eta}
is dependent on an unknown free surface
\eta
. To bypass the difficulty of working in such a domain, we proceed to reformulate the problem on a fixed domain
\Omega_{b}=\Sigma\times(0,b)
, at the cost of worsening the nonlinearities in the system.

To do so we introduce the flattening map
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}:\overline{\Omega_{b}}\to\overline{\Omega_{b+\eta}}
associated to a continuous function
\eta:\Sigma_{b}\to(-b,\infty)
, defined via

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}(x^{\prime},x_{n})=x+\frac{x_{n}\eta(x^{\prime})}{b}e_{n}.

(1.17)

We note that by construction,
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}\rvert_{\Sigma_{0}}=\Sigma_{0}
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}(x^{\prime},b)=\Sigma_{\eta+b}
(see Figure
2
). Moreover,
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
is bijective with its inverse
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}^{-1}:\overline{\Omega_{b+\eta}}\to\overline{\Omega_{b}}
given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}^{-1}(y)=\left(y^{\prime},\frac{by_{n}}{b+\eta(x^{\prime})}\right).

(1.18)

Figure 2.
The flattening map
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
and its inverse

Throughout the rest of the paper we will typically suppress the dependence of the subsequent maps and their associated domains on
b
and
\eta
, e.g. writing
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
as
\mathfrak{F}
and
\Omega_{b}
as
\Omega
, unless these dependencies need to be emphasized. We also use the following abuse of notation throughout the paper: since the hypersurface
\Sigma_{b}\subset\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}
is canonically diffeomorphic to
\Sigma
via the projection map
\pi:\Sigma_{b}\to\Sigma
given by
\pi(x^{\prime},b)=x^{\prime}
, we will use this to identify
H^{s}(\Sigma_{b};V)
with
H^{s}(\Sigma;V)
(and similar spaces) for any finite dimensional vector space
V
. This allows us to sometimes write
f(x^{\prime})
instead of
f(x^{\prime},b)
, and allows us to apply the horizontal Fourier transform on
\Sigma_{b}
in the natural way.

Following this convention, we note that
\mathfrak{F}
is a homeomorphism inheriting the regularity of
\eta
, in the sense that if
\eta\in C^{k}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
then
\mathfrak{F}
is a
C^{k}
diffeomorphism. When
\eta
is differentiable we may compute

\displaystyle\nabla\mathfrak{F}(x)=\begin{pmatrix}I_{(n-1)\times(n-1)}&0_{(n-1)\times 1}\\
\frac{x_{n}\nabla^{\prime}\eta(x^{\prime})^{T}}{b}&1+\frac{\eta(x^{\prime})}{b}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.19)

Thus, the Jacobian
\mathcal{J}
and the inverse Jacobian
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathfrak{F}
are

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}=\det\nabla\mathfrak{F}=1+\frac{\eta}{b},\quad\mathcal{K}=\frac{1}{J}=\frac{b}{b+\eta}.

(1.20)

We then introduce the matrix
\mathcal{A}:\Omega_{b}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
defined via

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(x)=(\nabla\mathfrak{F})^{-\intercal}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{(n-1)\times(n-1)}&\frac{-x_{n}\nabla^{\prime}\eta(x^{\prime})}{b+\eta(x^{\prime})}\\
0_{1\times(n-1)}&\frac{b}{b+\eta(x^{\prime})}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{(n-1)\times(n-1)}&-x_{n}\mathcal{K}\frac{\nabla^{\prime}\eta(x^{\prime})}{b}\\
0_{1\times(n-1)}&\mathcal{K}\end{pmatrix},

(1.21)

the
\mathcal{A}
-dependent differential operators

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}f)_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}f,\quad(X\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}u)_{i}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}X_{j}\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}u_{i},\quad\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}X=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}X_{i},

(1.22)

and also

(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u)_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{ik}\partial_{k}u_{j}+\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}u_{i},\quad S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)=pI-\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u,\\
\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)=\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}p-\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}u-\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}u,\quad(\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}u)_{i}=\sum_{j,k,m=1}^{n}\mathcal{A}_{j,k}\partial_{k}(\mathcal{A}_{jm}\partial_{m}u_{i}).

(1.23)

Now writing
u,U^{1},U^{2}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n},p:\Omega\to\mathbb{R},f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
via
u=v\circ\mathfrak{F},U^{1}=W^{1}\circ\mathfrak{F},U^{2}=W^{2}\circ\mathfrak{F},p=q\circ\mathfrak{F}
, where
v,q
satisfies (
1.10
), (
1.16
) can be reformulated as the quasilinear system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)-\gamma e_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}u-\gamma\kappa(x_{n}+\eta\frac{x_{n}}{b})\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}+\left(u+U^{1}+U^{2}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\left(u+U^{1}+U^{2}\right)&\\
\hskip 21.68121pt+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)-\kappa\left(x_{n}+\eta\frac{x_{n}}{b}\right)\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{1}-\kappa((x_{n}+\eta\frac{x_{n}}{b})\nabla^{\prime}\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta)=\mathfrak{f}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta},&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}u+\kappa\left(x_{n}+\eta(x^{\prime})\frac{x_{n}}{b}\right)\partial_{1}\eta=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
u\cdot\mathcal{N}=\left(-\gamma+s(\eta+b)+\kappa(\eta+b)\eta\right)\partial_{1}\eta,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\mathcal{N}=\left[-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\eta)I+\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}+\kappa(\eta+b)(e_{1}\otimes(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)\otimes e_{1})\right]\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.24)

1.6.
Statement of main results

We now state the main results obtained in this paper, though we delay a thorough discussion of them to the next subsection. The first result concerns a key property of the anisotropic Sobolev space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
, defined later in Section
2.1
for
\mathbb{F}\in\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}\}
, which serves as the container space for the free surface function
\eta
when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 1.1

(Proven later in Section
2.2
)
.

Suppose
s>d/2
, where
d=\dim\Sigma
and
\Sigma
is defined via (
1.1
). Then the anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
defined in Definition
2.5
is an algebra.

The proof of this theorem utilizes anisotropic Littlewood-Paley techniques heavily inspired by
[
11
]
.
We note that this theorem is only non-trivial in the case when
1\in R_{\Sigma},|R_{\Sigma}|\geqslant 1
, where the set
R_{\Sigma}
is defined via (
A.2
). It turns out that in all the remaining possibilities of
\Sigma
, the anisotropic Sobolev
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
coincides with the standard Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
, so the result follows directly from standard Sobolev theory. In Lemma
2.2
we give the precise characterization of product domains
\Gamma
for which
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})=X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
and
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\subsetneq X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. Moreover, we show in Proposition
2.3
that if
\Gamma=L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, then the space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is not complete, which is an initial indication of why we cannot allow for
\Sigma=L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
in our analysis.

With Theorem
1.1
in hand, we are able to prove the solvability of flattened system (
1.24
), and by extension the solvability of the original system (
1.16
). Before stating the solvability results we make a quick comment on the forcing terms in (
1.16
) and (
1.24
). We first note that the bulk force
\mathfrak{f}
in (
1.16
) needs to be defined in the domain
\Omega_{b+\eta}
, which depends on the unknown free surface function
\eta
. In order to work with
\mathfrak{f}
without the implicit need to know
\eta
first, we will assume a priori that
\mathfrak{f}
is defined in all of
\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}
. We will also need the map
(\mathfrak{f},\eta)\mapsto\mathfrak{f}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
to be
C^{1}
, since we need to invoke the implicit function theorem in our analysis in Section
4
. It is known that this is possible, for example, if the domain of
\mathfrak{f}
is
H^{s+1}
and its codomain is
H^{s}
. Due to the strip-like structure of the fluid domain and the flattening map defined in (
1.17
), we can in fact allow for another type of bulk force which preserves the regularity of its domain. A detailed discussion of this can be found in Section 1.4 of
[
18
]
. Moving forward, we consider the generalized bulk forces
\mathfrak{f}+L_{\Omega_{b+\eta}}f
and the generalized surface forces
\mathcal{T}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b+\eta}}+S_{b+\eta}T
in (
1.16
), where
L_{\Omega_{\eta+b}}f(x^{\prime},x_{n})=f(x^{\prime})
and
S_{b+\eta}T(x^{\prime},x_{n})=T(x^{\prime})
.

With this in mind, we are able to prove the solvability of (
1.24
).
The spaces
C^{k}_{b},C^{k}_{0},{}_{0}H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
referred to in the following results are defined in Section
1.8
, and the space
\mathcal{X}^{s}
is defined in Definition
3.10
. We also write
\Sigma\ni x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}_{R_{\Sigma}}+x^{\prime}_{T_{\Sigma}}
in accordance with the notation introduced in (
A.1
).

Theorem 1.2

(Proved later in Section
4.2
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be given by (
1.1
) and suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s>\frac{n}{2}
. Further suppose that either
\sigma>0
and
n\geqslant 2
or else
\sigma=0
and
n=2
. Then there exists open sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{s}\subset(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}\times H^{s+2}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym})\times H^{s+1}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})

(1.25)

and
\mathcal{O}^{s}\subset\mathcal{X}^{s}
such that the following hold.

(1)

(0,0,0)\in\mathcal{O}^{s}
, and for every
(u,p,\eta)\in\mathcal{O}^{s}
we have that

\displaystyle u\in C_{b}^{2+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}),p\in C^{1+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}_{b}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}),\eta\in C^{3+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})

(1.26)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{|x_{R_{\Sigma}}^{\prime}|\to\infty}\partial^{\alpha}u(x)

\displaystyle=0\;\text{for all}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\;\text{such that}\;|\alpha|\leqslant 2+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor

(1.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{|x_{R_{\Sigma}}^{\prime}|\to\infty}\partial^{\alpha}p(x)

\displaystyle=0\;\text{for all}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\;\text{such that}\;|\alpha|\leqslant 1+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor,

(1.28)

\max_{\Sigma}|\eta|\leqslant\frac{b}{2}
, and the flattening map
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and a
C^{3+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}
diffeomorphism.

(2)

We have
(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}\subset\mathcal{U}^{s}

(3)

For each
(\gamma,\kappa,\mathcal{T},T,\mathfrak{f},f)\in\mathcal{U}^{s}
, there exists a unique
(u,p,\eta)\in\mathcal{O}^{s}
classically solving

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)-\gamma e_{1}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}u-\gamma\kappa(x_{n}+\eta\frac{x_{n}}{b})\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}+\left(u+U^{1}+U^{2}\right)\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\left(u+U^{1}+U^{2}\right)&\\
\hskip 21.68121pt+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)-\kappa\left(x_{n}+\eta\frac{x_{n}}{b}\right)\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{1}-\kappa((x_{n}+\eta\frac{x_{n}}{b})\nabla^{\prime}\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta)=\mathfrak{f}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}+L_{\Omega_{b}}f,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge_{\mathcal{A}}u+\kappa\left(x_{n}+\eta(x^{\prime})\frac{x_{n}}{b}\right)\partial_{1}\eta=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
u\cdot\mathcal{N}=\left(-\gamma+s(\eta+b)+\kappa(\eta+b)\eta\right)\partial_{1}\eta,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\mathcal{N}=\left[-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\eta)I+\mathcal{T}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}|_{\Sigma_{b}}+S_{b}T+\kappa(\eta+b)(e_{1}\otimes(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)\otimes e_{1})\right]\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.29)

(4)

The map
\mathcal{U}^{s}\ni(\gamma,\kappa,\mathcal{T},T,\mathfrak{f},f)\mapsto(u,p,\eta)\in\mathcal{O}^{s}
is
C^{1}
and locally Lipschitz.

A few remarks are in order. First, we note again that in the physically relevant case
n=3
, we cannot solve (
1.29
) in the stated spaces if
\Sigma=L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
. Second, Theorem
1.2
is analogous to Theorem 1.1 in
[
18
]
for the problem without incline and periodicity, and Theorem 1 in
[
25
]
for the multilayer problem. However, our choice of the function space
\mathcal{X}^{s}
is slightly different from the one used there because we have formulated the problem (
1.29
) in a slightly different manner, with the
\eta
term shifted from the dynamic boundary condition into the bulk. This results in the pressure belonging to a standard Sobolev space rather than a specialized anisotropic one, as in
[
18
,
25
]
. Third, we can say something about the set of
(\gamma,\kappa)
parameters for which we can produce solutions,

\mathfrak{P}^{s}=\{(\gamma,\kappa)\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}\mid(\gamma,\kappa,\mathcal{T},T,\mathfrak{f},f)\in\mathcal{U}^{s}\text{ for some }(\mathcal{T},T,\mathfrak{f},f)\}.

(1.30)

Indeed, an examination of our proof of Theorem
1.2
shows that for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
, there exists a
\kappa_{0}(\gamma)>0
, depending on
\gamma
and the other physical parameters in a semi-explicit way (see Theorem
3.19
), such that

(-\kappa_{0}(\gamma),\kappa_{0}(\gamma))\subseteq\{\kappa\in\mathbb{R}\mid(\gamma,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{P}^{s}\}.

(1.31)

However, the estimates in Theorem
3.19
suggest that for each
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
, the set
\{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}\mid(\gamma,\kappa)\in\mathfrak{P}^{s}\}
is bounded, and possibly empty for large
|\kappa|
. We conjecture that this is indeed the case, but we do not have a complete proof here due to the complicated dependence the operator norm of
L_{0,\sigma}
, defined in (
3.65
), on
\gamma
.

Using solutions constructed in the flattened domain via Theorem
1.2
, we can then produce solutions to the unflattened system (
1.16
). This leads us to the final result of this paper.

Theorem 1.3

(Proved later in Section
4.3
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be given by (
1.1
), and suppose that
\mathbb{N}\ni s>\frac{n}{2}
. Suppose that either
\sigma>0
and
n\geqslant 2
or else
\sigma=0
and
n=2
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{s}\subset(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}\times H^{s+2}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}_{\sym})\times H^{s+1}(\Sigma\times\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})

(1.32)

and
\mathcal{O}^{s}\subset\mathcal{X}^{s}
be the same open sets as in Theorem
1.2
. Then for each
(\gamma,\kappa,\mathcal{T},T,\mathfrak{f},f)\in\mathcal{U}^{s}
, there exist

\bullet

a free surface function
\eta\in X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\cap C_{0}^{\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor+2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
such that
\max_{\Sigma}|\eta|\leqslant b/2
, and
\mathfrak{F}_{\eta}
as defined in (
1.17
) a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism and a
C^{\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor+3}
diffeomorphism,

\bullet

a velocity field
v\in{}_{0}H^{s+2}(\Omega_{b+\eta};\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C^{\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor+2}(\Omega_{b+\eta};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\bullet

a pressure
q\in H^{s+1}(\Omega_{b+\eta};\mathbb{R})\cap C_{b}^{\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor+1}(\Omega_{b+\eta};\mathbb{R})
,

\bullet

and constants
C,R>0
,

such that the following hold:

(1)

(v,q,\eta)
are classical solutions to

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(q,v)-\gamma e_{1}\cdot\nabla v-\gamma\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}+(v+W^{1}+W^{2})\cdot\nabla(v+W^{1}+W^{2})+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)\\
\hskip 21.68121pt-\kappa x_{n}\Delta^{\prime}\eta(x^{\prime})e_{1}-\kappa(x_{n}\nabla^{\prime}\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta)=\mathfrak{f}+L_{\Omega_{b+\eta}}f,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\eta}\\
\diverge{v}+\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta(x^{\prime})=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega_{b+\eta}\\
(-\gamma+s(\eta+b)+\kappa(\eta+b)\eta)\partial_{1}\eta=v\cdot\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\eta}\\
S(q,v)\mathcal{N}=[-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\eta)I+\mathcal{T}|_{\Sigma_{b+\eta}}+S_{b+\eta}T+\kappa(b+\eta)(e_{1}\otimes(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)\otimes e_{1})]\mathcal{N},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b+\eta}\\
v=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.33)

(2)

(v\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta},q\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta},\eta)\in\mathcal{O}^{s}\subset\mathcal{X}^{s}
,

(3)

If
(\gamma_{*},\kappa_{\ast},\mathcal{T}_{*},T_{*},\mathfrak{f}_{*},f_{*})\in U^{s}
satisfy

\displaystyle|\gamma-\gamma_{*}|+|\kappa-\kappa_{\ast}|+\left\|\mathcal{T}-\mathcal{T}_{*}\right\|_{H^{s+2}}+\left\|T-T_{*}\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}}+\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{f}_{*}\right\|_{H^{s+1}}+\left\|f-f_{*}\right\|_{H^{s}}<R,

(1.34)

then the corresponding solution triple
(v_{*},q_{*},\eta_{*})
satisfy

\left\|(v\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta},q\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta},\eta)-(v_{*}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta_{\ast}},q_{*}\circ\mathfrak{F}_{\eta_{\ast}},\eta_{*})\right\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s}}\\
\leqslant C(|\gamma-\gamma_{*}|+|\kappa-\kappa_{\ast}|+\left\|\mathcal{T}-\mathcal{T}_{*}\right\|_{H^{s+2}}+\left\|T-T_{*}\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}}+\left\|\mathfrak{f}-\mathfrak{f}_{*}\right\|_{H^{s+1}}+\left\|f-f_{*}\right\|_{H^{s}}).

(1.35)

1.7.
Discussion of techniques and plan of attack

As in
[
18
]
, our strategy for producing solutions to (
1.24
) is to employ an implicit function theorem argument built on the linearization of (
1.24
),

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u-\gamma\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)+s(x_{n})\partial_{1}u\\
\hskip 21.68121pt+s^{\prime}(x_{n})u_{n}e_{1}+\kappa x_{n}s(x_{n})\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}-\kappa x_{n}\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{1}-\kappa(x_{n}\nabla^{\prime}\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta)=f,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge u+\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta=g,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
u_{n}+(\gamma-\frac{\kappa b^{2}}{2})\partial_{1}\eta=h,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
S(p,u)e_{n}+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n}=k,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(1.36)

When
\kappa=0
and
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, the strategy for producing solutions to (
1.16
) and is discussed extensively in Section 1.5 of
[
18
]
. To succinctly summarize their approach, a viable strategy for producing solutions to (
1.4
) is to use the implicit function theorem after first proving that (
1.36
) induces an isomorphism
(u,p,\eta)\mapsto(f,g,h,k)
between a pair of identified function spaces. Since (
1.36
) is not a standard elliptic system in the sense considered in Agmon-Douglis-Nirenberg
[
1
]
, as the unknown free surface
\eta
only appears in the equations imposed on the boundary, we follow the decoupling strategy in
[
18
]
and first study the overdetermined system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u=f&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge u=g,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
S(p,u)e_{n}=k,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u_{n}=h,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}\end{cases}

(1.37)

and its associated compatibility conditions. By the adjoint compatibility condition (
3.25
), the free surface function
\eta
can be constructed from the data tuple
(f,g,h,k)
by means of the pseudodifferential equation
\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)\hat{\eta}(\xi)=\psi(\xi)
for
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
, where
\hat{\Sigma}
is the dual group of
\Sigma
defined via (
1.45
), and
\psi,\rho_{\gamma}
are defined in (
3.35
), (
3.19
) as

\psi(\xi)=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\right)\;dx_{n}-\hat{k}(\xi)\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\hat{h}(\xi),\\
\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)=2\pi i\gamma\xi_{1}+(1+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\sigma)\overline{m(\xi,-\gamma)}.

(1.38)

Here
V,Q,m
, defined via (
3.9
), are symbols of special pseudodifferential operators. In order to solve the pseudodifferential equation for
\eta
, we need the precise asymptotics of
V,Q,m
on
\hat{\Sigma}
. In particular, we note that when
\gamma\neq 0
and
\Sigma
is defined via (
1.1
), the asymptotics of
m
completely determine the asymptotics of
\rho_{\gamma}
on
\hat{\Sigma}
, which for
\gamma\neq 0
are given in Lemma
3.6
as

\displaystyle\begin{cases}|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}\asymp(\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+(1+|\xi|^{2})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\xi),&\sigma>0\\
|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}\asymp|\xi|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+(1+|\xi|^{2})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\xi),&\sigma=0,n=2.\end{cases}

(1.39)

With the asymptotics of
\rho_{\gamma}
in hand, we define
\hat{\eta}
via
\hat{\eta}(\xi)=\psi(\xi)/\rho(\xi)
for
\xi\neq 0
, which by (
1.39
) would imply

\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4}}{|\xi|^{2}}|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+5/2}|\eta(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}\;d\xi\\
\asymp\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\frac{|\psi(\xi)|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+3/2}|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}\;d\xi.

(1.40)

Using the asymptotics of
V,Q
in Theorem
3.5
, and the functional framework built on the specialized anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
that serves as the container space for the free surface function
\eta
in Section
2.1
, we can then utilize the equivalence (
1.40
) to recover
\eta
via Fourier reconstruction. We note that by the first items of Lemma
2.2
and Theorem
2.6
, the free surface function
\eta
recovered through this process will be regular enough to be a classical function as opposed just being a tempered distribution, which is a crucial requirement for
\eta
to be utilized in the subsequent nonlinear analysis.

It is also worth mentioning that in the physically relevant dimension
n=3
, we deliberately chose to ignore the configuration
\Sigma=(L_{1}\mathbb{T})\times\mathbb{R}
, as in this case we would have
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Sigma}}(0,r)\Longleftrightarrow\xi=(0,\xi_{2}),|\xi_{2}|\leqslant r
for
r=\min(1,L_{1}^{-1})
. By (
1.38
), this implies that

|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}}(0,r)}\asymp|\xi|^{4}\implies|\xi|^{2}|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}}(0,r)}\asymp\frac{|\psi(\xi)|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}}(0,r)}.

Unfortunately, in this scenario we only have
\dot{H}^{1}
control over the low frequency modes of
\eta
, and by Proposition
2.3
the corresponding anisotropic space
X^{s}((L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R});\mathbb{R})
fails to be complete. If one were to consider the completion of this space, elements of the completion would be equivalence classes of tempered distributions modulo polynomials. Since we mandate elements of the container space for the free surface function
\eta
to be classical functions, neither the space
X^{s}((L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R});\mathbb{R})
nor its completion are suitable for the purposes of this paper. Practically speaking, this means that we cannot employ our techniques assuming a priori periodicity in the direction of incline. For the same reason, our framework also cannot produce stationary solutions in the case when
\gamma=0
.

By solving for
\eta
through the aforementioned approach, we can then solve for
(u,p)
by means of (
1.36
) and the linear isomorphism associated to (
1.37
). Fortunately, this is possible as the results from the linear analysis in
[
18
]
continue to hold over
\Sigma
defined in (
1.1
). This is mainly due to the fact that many results in
[
18
]
are proved by analyzing the low frequency behavior of functions in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
and
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
. By Remark
A.6
and Remark
3.2
, the analogous
results for
H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
and
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
can all be deduced from reducing a similar set of calculations over
\Sigma
and
\hat{\Sigma}
to the calculations in
[
18
]
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As such, the results from
[
18
]
can be directly ported over with minimal modification of the proofs contained therein.

Though, as seen in the case
\Sigma=L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, the generalized space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
can fail to be complete depending on the set
\Gamma
over which it is defined. This is an initial indication that some care needs to be taken in generalizing the space
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
introduced in
[
18
]
over general sets
\Gamma
. We devote the Appendix to developing the necessary tools to carefully define the space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
over product domains
\Gamma
in Section
2.1
, and also identify the “good sets”
\Sigma
for which the spaces
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
are compatible with our analysis, which led us to (
1.1
).

Next, we discuss the role of the perturbations (
1.9
), (
1.15
), and in particular the role of (
1.14
). First, by renormalizing the pressure via (
1.8
), the vertical gravitational force
-e_{n}
shifts from the bulk to the dynamic boundary conditions, appearing as the term
\zeta\nu
on the right hand side of the fourth equation in (
1.10
); this term later appears as
\eta\mathcal{N}
in the fourth equation of (
1.12
). By further modifying the pressure via (
1.15
), we eliminate the term
\eta\mathcal{N}
from (
1.12
), at the price of introducing
(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)
to the bulk in (
1.16
) and (
1.24
). The advantage of this formulation is that the pressure
q
in Theorem
1.3
lives in a standard Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
, as opposed to the alternative formulation in
[
18
]
, for which
q
belongs to a specialized Sobolev space built on the anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
.

A key difference between the system (
1.10
) and the analogous system in
[
18
]
is that upon removing the background shear flow (
1.7
), we are left with the term
\kappa\eta(e_{1}\otimes e_{n}+e_{n}\otimes e_{1})\mathcal{N}
appearing in the fourth equation of (
1.12
). Since
\eta
is expected to belong to a specialized Sobolev space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
that does not coincide with the standard Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
in general, attacking the problem at this level would require one to build specialized spaces for the data tuple
(f,g,h,k)
, and also prove that the associated linear maps remain to be isomorphisms in this modified functional framework. By introducing an additional perturbation (
1.14
), we were able to replace this term with terms that are all standard Sobolev in the regime
s>n/2
thanks to the second item of Theorem
2.6
, as the non-trivial terms are either derivatives of functions in
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
or products of functions in
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
and derivatives of functions in
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. This approach allows us to directly employ the functional framework from
[
18
]
at the price of introducing worse nonlinearities in (
1.16
) and (
1.24
).

To construct solutions to (
1.16
), we use the associated linear isomorphism in conjunction with the implicit function theorem around the trivial solution. In order to invoke the implicit function theorem, the nonlinear maps associated to (
1.24
) first need to be well-defined on the same spaces used in the linear analysis. This requires some analysis in Section
4.1
to understand the mapping properties of functions in the specialized Sobolev space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, and also of the mean-curvature operator
\mathcal{H}
defined in (
1.2
).

In addition, we also need a divergence-trace type compatibility condition (
3.22
) built into the container space
\mathcal{Y}^{s}
defined in (
3.23
) to hold; in Theorem
4.2
, we show that the compatibility condition requires

\left[-s(\eta+b)\partial_{1}\eta-\kappa(\eta+b)\eta\partial_{1}\eta-\int_{0}^{b}J(x^{\prime},x_{n})\kappa\left(x_{n}+\eta(x^{\prime})\frac{x_{n}}{b}\right)\partial_{1}\eta(\cdot)\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\\
=\left[-\kappa(b^{2}\partial_{1}\eta+b\partial_{1}\eta^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\partial_{1}\eta^{3})\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}<\infty,

(1.41)

where the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm is defined in (
3.2
).

A major obstacle in proving (
1.41
) is that low-frequency control of powers of
\eta
is not immediately evident from the inclusion
\eta\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. One could in principle attempt to obtain control of powers of
\eta
by way of Young’s convolution inequality applied on the Fourier side. However, in the model case
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
s>d/2
, this only leads to the inclusion
\eta^{k}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
for
k\geqslant 2+\lceil 4/(2d-2)\rceil,d\geqslant 2
. Unfortunately, this means that in the physically relevant case
n=3
(so that
d=n-1=2
), this elementary argument only provides control over quartic or higher powers of
\eta
. However, by the fourth item of Theorem
2.6
, we know that if
\zeta\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, then
\partial_{1}\zeta\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. Thus, a viable strategy for proving (
1.41
) is to show that
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is an algebra. By Lemma
2.2
, there are configurations of
\Sigma
for which
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})=H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, in which case we know
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is an algebra for
s>\dim\Sigma/2
. In general, though, we only know that
H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, so further analysis is required to show that
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is an algebra. Fortunately, we are able to establish this in Theorem
1.1
, which is proved later in Section
2.2
.

As the linearization of (
1.24
) depends on
\kappa
, we also need to identify a parameter regime for
\kappa
for which the associated linear map remain an isomorphism. To that end, in Section
3.6
we study the map

L_{\kappa,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)=(\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}-\gamma\partial_{1}u+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)+s(x_{n})\partial_{1}u+s^{\prime}(x_{n})u_{n}e_{1}+\kappa x_{n}s(x_{n})\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}\\
-\kappa x_{n}\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{1}-\kappa(x_{n}\partial_{1}\nabla^{\prime}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta),\diverge u+\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta,u_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+(\gamma-\frac{\kappa b^{2}}{2})\partial_{1}\eta,S(p,u)e_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n})

(1.42)

induced by the linearization associated to (
1.24
), and in Theorem
3.19
we show that via a perturbative argument around
\kappa=0
that for fixed
\gamma
and other physical parameters, there exists a
\kappa_{0}>0
for which
L_{\kappa,\sigma}
is an isomorphism over appropriate spaces for all
\kappa\in(-\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0})
. Synthesizing the aforementioned results and employing our strategy of invoking the implicit function theorem leads us to the solvability of (
1.24
) and (
1.16
).

Finally, we discuss the strategy for producing solutions to the unflattened system (
1.16
) using solutions constructed in the flattened domain via Theorem
1.2
. To that end, we use the free surface function
\eta
to build the flattening map and its inverse defined in (
1.17
) and (
1.18
) to undo the reformulation outlined in Section
1.5
. This requires some results on the regularity of these maps, which is recorded in Section
4.1
. Fortunately, the same analysis can be adapted from
[
18
]
with minimal modification.

1.8.
Notational conventions and outline of article

In this subsection we discuss the notational conventions adopted throughout this paper. In this paper,
\mathbb{N}
denotes the natural numbers including 0. We always use
n\geqslant 1
to denote the dimension of the flattened fluid domain
\Omega=\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}
. For
d\geqslant 1
, we will consider spaces defined over domains of the form

\displaystyle\Gamma=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\Gamma_{i},\;\Gamma_{i}=\mathbb{R}\;\text{or}\;L_{i}\mathbb{T},\;L_{i}>0,

(1.43)

where
\Gamma
is endowed with the natural group, topological, and smooth structures. In fact, the topology on
\Gamma
is metrizable, and in this paper we equip
\Gamma
with the metric

\displaystyle d_{\Gamma}(x,y)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})^{2}\right)^{1/2}\text{ for }d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})=\begin{cases}d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})=|x_{i}-y_{i}|,&i\in R_{\Gamma}\\
d_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})=\inf\{|r-s|\mid r\in[x_{i}],\;s\in[y_{i}]\},&i\in T_{\Gamma}.\end{cases}

(1.44)

We write
\hat{\Gamma}
to denote the dual group associated to
\Gamma
, defined via

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}=\widehat{\prod_{i=1}^{d}\Gamma_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\hat{\Gamma}_{i},\;\hat{\Gamma}_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R},&\Gamma_{i}=\mathbb{R}\\
L_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{Z},&\Gamma_{i}=L_{i}\mathbb{T},\end{cases}

(1.45)

where
\hat{\Gamma}
is also endowed with the obvious group, topological, and smooth structures. We also endow
\hat{\Gamma}
with the metric induced by inclusion in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle d_{\hat{\Gamma}}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(|x_{i}-y_{i}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(1.46)

If
X
is a metric space, we write
B_{X}(x,r)=\{y\in X\mid d_{X}(x,y)<r\}
for the open ball.

For
X\in\{\Gamma,\hat{\Gamma}\}
, we write
\mathscr{S}(X;\mathbb{C})
to denote the Schwartz class of complex valued functions over
X
and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(X;\mathbb{C})
to denote the space of complex valued tempered distributions over
X
; a detailed treatment of how to define these space can be found in Appendix
A.2
. For
f\in\mathscr{S}(X;\mathbb{C})
or
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(X;\mathbb{C})
, we denote its unitary Fourier and inverse Fourier transforms by

\displaystyle\hat{f}(\xi)=\mathscr{F}_{X}^{+}\{f\}(\xi),\quad\check{f}(\xi)=\mathscr{F}_{X}^{-}\{f\}(\xi),

(1.47)

where
\mathscr{F}_{X}^{\pm}
is defined in (
A.19
). Sometimes we will also write

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}[f](\xi)=\mathscr{F}_{X}^{+}\{f\}(\xi),\quad\mathscr{F}^{-1}[f](\xi)=\mathscr{F}_{X}^{-}\{f\}(\xi).

(1.48)

For
\mathbb{R}\ni s>1/2
and a Lipschitz
\upzeta:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying
\inf\upzeta>0
, we define

\displaystyle{}_{0}H^{s}(\Omega_{\upzeta};\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{u\in H^{s}(\Omega_{\upzeta};\mathbb{R}^{n})\mid u=0\;\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}\},

(1.49)

where the equality is taken in the trace sense. In the case when
s=1
and
\zeta=b
, we endow
{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega_{b};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the inner product

\displaystyle(u,v)_{{}_{0}H^{1}}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathbb{D}u:\mathbb{D}v\;dx,

(1.50)

which generates the same topology as the standard
H^{1}
-norm by Korn’s inequality (see Lemma 2.7 of
[
3
]
). For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a real Banach space
V
, and a nonempty open set
U\subseteq\Gamma
we define the space

\displaystyle C^{k}(U;V)=\{f:U\to V\mid Lf\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{d};V),\;Lf(x)=f([x])\}.

(1.51)

We define

\displaystyle C^{k}_{b}(U;V)=\{f:U\to V\mid f\in C^{k}(U;V)\;\text{and}\;\left\|f\right\|_{C^{k}_{b}}<\infty\},

(1.52)

where

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{C^{k}_{b}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d},|\alpha|\leqslant k}\sup_{x\in U}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f(x)\right\|_{V}.

(1.53)

We also define the space
C_{0}^{k}(\Gamma;V)\subset C^{k}_{b}(\Gamma;V)
to be the closed subspace

\displaystyle C^{k}_{0}(\Gamma;V)=\{f\in C^{k}_{b}(\Gamma;V)\mid\lim_{|x_{R_{\Gamma}}|\to\infty}\partial^{\alpha}f(x)=0\;\text{for all}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\;\text{such that}\;|\alpha|\leqslant k\},

(1.54)

where
x_{R_{\Gamma}}\in\Gamma
is defined via (
A.2
). Lastly, we use
L^{0}(\Gamma;[0,\infty])
to denote the set of non-negative measurable functions over
\Gamma
.

We conclude this section by giving outline of the article. In Section
2
, we introduce the anisotropic Sobolev space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
and characterize the space based on the underlying product structure of the domain
\Gamma
. We then state its essential properties, and in Section
2.2
we prove that for
\Sigma
defined via (
1.1
),
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is an algebra in the supercritical regime
s>\dim\Sigma/2
.

In Section
3
, we first record the isomorphism associated to the overdetermined system (
1.37
) and the asymptotics of the special pseudodifferential symbols used in the construction of the free surface function
\eta
. This allows us to prove the isomorphism associated to the linear system (
1.36
). We then establish the parameter regime for
\kappa
for which the flattened system (
1.24
) induces an isomorphism.

In Section
4
, we first record some key mapping properties of the anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
and various nonlinear maps used in the subsequent analysis. Using these preliminary results, we show that the maps induced by (
1.24
) are well-defined and smooth, and we use this in conjunction with the implicit function theorem to produce solutions to (
1.24
). We conclude the paper by using the solutions from (
1.24
) to produce solutions to (
1.16
).

2.
The anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})

In this section we aim to generalize the anisotropic Sobolev space
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
introduced in Section 5 of
[
18
]
. First, in order to define such a space on more general product domains, we need to define a suitable notion of tempered distributions on
\Gamma
and its Pontryagin dual
\hat{\Gamma}
; a detailed treatment of this can be found in Appendix
A.2
. Second, in order to understand how these spaces depend on the structure of the product domain
\Gamma
, we study how they behave under permutations of the factors of
\Gamma
; this is treated in Appendix
A.1

2.1.
Definition of
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
and its general properties

In this subsection we let
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
,
\mathbb{N}\ni d\geqslant 1
, and we consider the domain
\Gamma
defined via (
1.43
). Recall that the Japanese bracket
\langle\cdot\rangle:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
is defined via
\langle\xi\rangle=(1+|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}
. In
[
18
]
, the anisotropic space
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
is defined in terms of the Fourier multiplier
\omega_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle\omega_{s}(\xi)=\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(\xi)\left(\frac{|\xi_{1}|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}+|\xi|^{2}\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(\xi)\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2s}.

(2.1)

In this paper, we use the same formula in (
2.1
) to define
\omega_{s}
on
\hat{\Gamma}
; in the purely toroidal case when
\hat{\Gamma}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, we also define
\omega_{s}(0)=1
so that
\omega_{s}
takes on the same value as the standard Sobolev multiplier
\langle\cdot\rangle^{2s}
at
\xi=0
. The function
\omega_{s}
is introduced due to its close relation to the symbol
\rho_{\gamma}
from (
1.38
). To see this, note that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1\asymp_{r}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{s}\asymp_{r}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{s-1}&\text{for }\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\\
\left\langle\xi\right\rangle\asymp_{r}|\xi|\asymp_{r}\frac{|\xi_{1}|}{|\xi|}+|\xi|&\text{for }\xi\in\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r),\end{cases}

(2.2)

and so this and (
1.39
) show that for
\xi\neq 0
we have the equivalences

\frac{|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2s}\asymp\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(\xi)\left(\frac{|\xi_{1}|^{2}}{|\xi|^{2}}+|\xi|^{2}\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(\xi)\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2s}=\omega_{s}(\xi)\\
\asymp\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(\xi)\left(\frac{|\xi_{1}|}{|\xi|}+|\xi|\right)^{2}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(s-1)}+\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(\xi)\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(s-1)}\asymp\left(\frac{|\xi_{1}|}{|\xi|}+|\xi|\right)^{2}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(s-1)},

(2.3)

where the implicit constants depend only on
r,n,\gamma,\sigma,b
.

The equivalence (
2.3
) suggests that we can give equivalent definitions of the space by either using
\omega_{s}
or the multiplier
\upmu:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle\upmu(\xi)=\begin{cases}\frac{|\xi_{1}|}{|\xi|}+|\xi|&\text{for }\xi\neq 0\\
1&\text{for }\xi=0.\end{cases}

(2.4)

We note that defining
\upmu(0)=1
is only relevant in the purely toroidal case when
\hat{\Gamma}=\prod_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
, and we do so that the restriction of
\upmu
over
\hat{\Gamma}
is well-defined and it takes on the same value as
\langle\cdot\rangle^{2s}
at
\xi=0
. We then define the specialized Sobolev space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
of order
s\geqslant 0
in terms of
\upmu
, to be the function space

\displaystyle X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})=\begin{cases}\{{f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})\mid\hat{f}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C}),\;f=\overline{f},\;\lVert\upmu\langle\cdot\rangle^{s-1}\hat{f}\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty}\},&\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}\\
\{{f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})\mid\hat{f}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C}),\;\lVert\upmu\langle\cdot\rangle^{s-1}\hat{f}\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty}\},&\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\end{cases}

(2.5)

with the norm associated to
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
defined by

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}=\lVert\upmu\langle\cdot\rangle^{s-1}\hat{f}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.6)

The definitions of the class of tempered distributions
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})
on
\Gamma
and the Fourier transform on
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})
are contained in Section
A.2
of the appendix.

First, we show that if a permutation
g\in S_{d}
leaves the first factor of
\Gamma
fixed, then the restriction of the induced map
\sigma_{g}
on
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is an isometric isomorphism between
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
and
X^{s}(g\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. The reordered Cartesian product
g\Gamma
and the map
\sigma_{g}
are defined in Definition
A.3
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be defined as in (
1.43
) and let
S_{d}
denote the symmetric group of permutations. Suppose
g\in S_{d}
satisfies
g(1)=1
, and let
g\Gamma
be the reordered Cartesian product defined as in the first item of Definition
A.3
,
\mathcal{P}_{g}:\Gamma\to g\Gamma
be the reordering map defined as in the second item of Definition
A.3
. Then the map
\sigma_{g}:X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\to X^{s}(g\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
defined via
\sigma_{g}(f)=f(\mathcal{P}_{g}^{-1}\cdot)
is an isometric isomorphism, with its inverse given by the map
\sigma_{g,\Gamma}^{-1}:X^{s}(g\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\to X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
defined via
\sigma_{g}^{-1}(f)=f(\mathcal{P}_{g}\cdot)
.

Proof.

We note that
\langle\cdot\rangle=\langle\mathcal{P}_{g}\cdot\rangle
, and if
g(1)=1
, then
\upmu(\cdot)=\upmu(\mathcal{P}_{g}\cdot)
. As a consequence, the Fourier multiplier
\upmu^{2}(\cdot)\langle\cdot\rangle^{2(s-1)}
is invariant under
\mathcal{P}_{g}
, therefore the desired conclusion follows from Lemma
A.11
.
∎

Next, we characterize the anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
in relation to the set
R_{\Gamma}
defined in the first item of Definition
A.2
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be defined as in (
1.43
) with
\dim\Gamma=d\geqslant 1
and let
R_{\Gamma}
be defined as in (
A.2
). Let
\Gamma_{\ast},\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}
be the canonical reordering of
\Gamma
and
\hat{\Gamma}
defined in (
A.8
), and let
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}:\Gamma\to\Gamma_{\ast},\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}:\hat{\Gamma}\to\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}
be the canonical reordering maps defined in (
A.10
). Then the following hold.

(1)

If
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
or
R_{\Gamma}=\{1\}
, then
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})=H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}}
and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{H^{s}}
are equivalent norms. In particular, this implies that if
s>d/2
, then
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is an algebra.

(2)

If
1\in R_{\Gamma}
and
|R_{\Gamma}|\geqslant 2
, then
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is not closed under rotations in the sense that for any orthogonal matrix
Q\in O(|R_{\Gamma}|)
is such that
|Qe_{1}\cdot e_{1}|<1
, there exists a function
f\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\setminus L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
such that
f(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cdot)\notin X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
, where
Q\oplus I:\Gamma_{*,R}\oplus\Gamma_{*,T}\to\Gamma_{*,R}\oplus\Gamma_{*,T}
maps
(x,y)
to
(Qx,y)
. In particular, this implies that
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\subsetneq X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
.

(3)

If
1\notin R_{\Gamma}
and
R_{\Gamma}\neq\varnothing
, then
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\subsetneq X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. Furthermore, for all
f\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}^{2}\asymp\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|\xi|^{2}\left\langle\xi\right\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi.

(2.7)

Proof.

We note that since
\upmu(\xi)\leqslant 1+|\xi|
, we always have
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})\subseteq X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
. To prove the first item, we note that if
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
, then we are in the purely toroidal case when
\Gamma=\prod_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}\mathbb{T}
. Here, the only low frequency mode
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)
is
\xi=0
. By the definition of
\upmu
at
\xi=0
in (
2.4
), we have
\upmu(0)=1=\langle 0\rangle^{2s}
. So by (
2.2
) we find that for all
\xi\in\hat{\Gamma},\upmu(\xi)\asymp\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}
and the desired conclusion follows. If
R_{\Gamma}=\{1\}
, then
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}\times\prod_{i=1}^{d-1}L_{i}\mathbb{T}
. In this case,
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\Longleftrightarrow\xi=(\xi_{1},0)
for
|\xi_{1}|<r
. Then for
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)
, we have
\upmu(\xi)=1+|\xi_{1}|
and
\langle\xi\rangle=(1+|\xi_{1}|^{2})^{1/2}
, which implies that
\upmu(\xi)\asymp_{r}\langle\xi\rangle\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}+\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}\mathbbm{1}_{B^{c}(0,r)}\asymp\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}
for all
\xi\in\hat{\Gamma}
, and the desired conclusion follows.

Next we proceed to prove the second item. Let the set
T_{\Gamma}
be defined as in (
A.3
). The case when
T_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
follows from the third item of Proposition 5.2 in
[
18
]
, so we assume here that
T_{\Gamma}\neq\varnothing
.

Since
|R_{\Gamma}|\geqslant 2
, by the third item of Proposition 5.2 in
[
18
]
, for every
Q\in O(|R_{\Gamma}|)
such that
|Qe_{1}\cdot e_{1}|<1
there exists a function
G\in(X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|};\mathbb{F})\cap C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|};\mathbb{F}))\setminus L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|};\mathbb{F})
such that
G(Q\cdot)\notin X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|};\mathbb{F})
. Let
G
be such a function,
\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}
be the canonical reordering map defined via (
A.10
),
\hat{\Gamma}_{R}
be the set defined via (
A.4
), and
\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}}
be the surjective map on
\hat{\Gamma}
defined via (
A.14
). We then consider the measurable function
F:\hat{\Gamma}\to\mathbb{C}
defined via

\displaystyle F(\xi)=\begin{cases}\hat{G}(\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi),&\xi\in\hat{\Gamma}_{R}\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(2.8)

where
\hat{\Gamma}_{R}
is defined as in (
A.4
). We note that by Lemma
A.6
,
\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}}
is an isometric measure-preserving group isomorphism between
\hat{\Gamma}_{*,R}
and
\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma_{*}}|}=\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}
. Then by Fubini’s theorem, it follows that

\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|F(\xi)|\;d\xi=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}_{*,R}}|\hat{G}(\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}\xi)|\;d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}}|\hat{G}(\omega)|\;d\omega<\infty,\;\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|F(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}}|\hat{G}(\omega)|^{2}\;d\omega=\infty.

(2.9)

We also note that the Fourier multiplier
\upmu^{2}(\cdot)\langle\cdot\rangle^{2(s-1)}
is invariant under
P_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, and since
\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}\circ\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}
, by the same calculations in (
A.48
) and by Lemma
A.6
we have

\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\upmu^{2}(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|F(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}_{*,R}}\upmu^{2}(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\hat{G}(\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}}\upmu(\omega)^{2}\langle\omega\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\hat{G}(\omega)|^{2}\;d\xi<\infty.

(2.10)

Hence, the function
f:=\check{F}\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\setminus L^{2}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. In particular,
f\notin H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. On the other hand, we have

\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Gamma}[f(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cdot)](\xi)=\int_{\Gamma}f(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}x)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\;dx=\int_{\Gamma_{\ast}}f(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)x)e^{-2\pi i\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi\cdot x}\;dx\\
=\int_{\Gamma_{\ast}}f(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{-1}x)e^{-2\pi i((Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi)\cdot x}\;dx=\int_{\Gamma}f(x)e^{-2\pi i(\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi)\cdot x}\;dx=F(\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi).

(2.11)

Then by Lemma
A.6
and Fubini’s theorem,

\left\|f(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma}\cdot)\right\|_{X^{s}(\Gamma)}^{2}=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}\upmu(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|F(\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}_{R}}\upmu(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\hat{G}(\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}(Q\oplus I)\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\\
=\int_{\hat{\Gamma}_{*,R}}\upmu(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\hat{G}(\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}(Q\oplus I)\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}}\upmu(\omega)\langle\omega\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\hat{G}(Q\omega)|\;d\omega=\infty.

(2.12)

This proves the second item.

To prove the last item, we note that if
1\notin R_{\Gamma}
and
R_{\Gamma}\neq\varnothing
, then
\Gamma_{1}=L_{1}\mathbb{T}
and
\Gamma\neq\prod_{i=1}^{d}L_{i}\mathbb{T}
. In this setting,
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\Longleftrightarrow\xi\in\Gamma_{R}
as
\xi_{T}=0
for the low frequencies and
B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\setminus\{0\}\neq\varnothing
. In particular, for
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)
we have
\xi_{1}=0
, which implies that
\upmu(\xi)=|\xi_{R_{\hat{\Gamma}}}|=|\xi|
for
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\setminus\{0\}
, so by (
2.2
), we arrive at (
2.7
). By (
2.7
), it also follows that
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\subsetneq X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
.
∎

Recall that our ultimate aim in introducing
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is to use it as the container space for the free surface function
\eta
in our study of the traveling wave problem. The upshot of Lemma
2.2
is that the precise structure of the space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is heavily dependent on the form of the domain
\Gamma
, and in particular, the properties of the set
R_{\Gamma}
. In the first case considered in the lemma,
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is the standard Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
, and therefore we may employ standard Sobolev tools in our subsequent analysis. In the second case, even though
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is not a standard Sobolev space, we will be able to prove that it enjoys many of the same properties as
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. However, in the third case, which includes the physically relevant case when
\Gamma=L_{1}\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, the space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is unfortunately unusable for our subsequent analysis due to a failure of completeness. To justify the last claim, we prove the following proposition.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be defined as in (
1.43
) and let
R_{\Gamma}
be defined as in (
A.2
). If
1\notin R_{\Gamma}
and
R_{\Gamma}\neq\varnothing
, then
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is complete if and only if
|R_{\Gamma}|>2
.

Proof.

The proof is a modification of Proposition 1.34 in
[
2
]
, which characterizes when the homogeneous Sobolev space
\dot{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is complete. We define the measure
\mu_{s}:=|\xi|^{2}\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}\;d\xi
, and denote by
L^{2}_{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
the complex valued functions in
L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma};\mu_{s})
.

First assume
|R_{\Gamma}|>2
, and suppose
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. Then by (
2.7
),
\{\hat{u}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}_{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
, and hence there exists a function
f\in L^{2}_{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
such that
u_{n}\to f
in
L^{2}_{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
. Recall that by (
A.13
), we have
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\Longleftrightarrow\xi\in\hat{\Gamma}_{R}
, where
\hat{\Gamma}_{R}
is defined in (
A.4
). Using Lemma
A.6
and the assumption
|R_{\Gamma}|>2
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|f(\xi)|\;d\xi\leqslant\left(\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|\xi|^{2}|f(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{B_{|R_{\Gamma}|}(0,r)}|\omega|^{-2}\;d\omega\right)^{1/2}<\infty,

(2.13)

where in the last equality we have used the isometric measure-preserving isomorphism
\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}}
between
B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)
and
B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}}(0,r)
. Since
\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}f
belongs to
H^{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
, with (
2.13
) we can infer that
f
defines a tempered distribution. Then
u:=\check{f}\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
and
u_{n}\to u
in
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. This shows that
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is complete.

Now assume
|R_{\Gamma}|\leqslant 2
, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is complete with respect to the
X^{s}
norm defined via (
2.6
). Consider a new norm on
X^{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{F})
given by
\left\|u\right\|_{*}=\left\|u\right\|_{X^{s}}+\left\|\hat{u}\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r))}
; this norm is well-defined since the definition of
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
requires
\hat{u}\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
for any function
u\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. We then claim that
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is also complete endowed with the
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{*}
norm. Indeed, suppose
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
(X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{*})
, then
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
(X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}})
and
\{\hat{u}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r);\mathbb{C})
. By the assumed completeness of
(X^{s}(\Gamma,\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}})
, there exists a function
u\in(X^{s}(\Gamma,\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}})
for which
u_{n}\to u
in
(X^{s}(\Gamma,\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}})
. Similarly, there exists a function
g\in L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r);\mathbb{C})
for which
\hat{u}_{n}\to g
in
L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r);\mathbb{C})
. Clearly,
g=\hat{u}
a.e. in
L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r);\mathbb{C})
, therefore we can conclude that
u_{n}\to u
in
(X^{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{*})
. This completes the proof of the claim.

We now know that
X^{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{F})
is complete with respect to both
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}}
and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{*}
. The identity map
I:(X^{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{*})

\to(X^{s}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{F}),\left\|\cdot\right\|_{X^{s}})
is trivially continuous, thus we can invoke the bounded inverse theorem to deduce the existence of a universal constant
C>0
such that
\left\|u\right\|_{*}\leqslant C\left\|u\right\|_{X^{s}}
for all functions
u\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
. In turn, this implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{u}\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r))}\leqslant C\left\|u\right\|_{X^{s}}\;\text{for all}\;u\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F}).

(2.14)

To derive a contradiction, we construct an explicit function
f\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
for which (
2.14
) is violated. For any
\xi\in\hat{\Gamma}
, we adopt the convention in (
A.1
) and write
\xi=\xi_{R_{\hat{\Gamma}}}+\xi_{T_{\hat{\Gamma}}}
. Let
\mathcal{C}=\{\xi\in\hat{\Gamma}\mid\xi_{T_{\hat{\Gamma}}}=0\;\text{and}\;r/2<|\xi_{R_{\hat{\Gamma}}}|<r\}
, which in particular implies that
2^{-i}\mathcal{C}\cap 2^{-j}\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
for any
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
i\neq j
. Now for every
n\geqslant 1
we then consider
G_{n}\in L^{1}(\hat{\Gamma};\mathbb{C})
defined via

\displaystyle G_{n}(\xi)=\sum_{q=1}^{n}\frac{2^{q(1+|R_{\Gamma}|/2)}}{q}\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-q}\mathcal{C}}(\xi).

(2.15)

We note in particular that
\overline{G_{n}(\xi)}=G_{n}(-\xi)
and
\supp G_{n}\subseteq B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)\subset\hat{\Gamma}
, and so we may define the smooth and bounded function
g_{n}:\Gamma_{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
via
g_{n}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[G_{n}]
. Let
\mathscr{H}
denote the Hausdorff measure over
\hat{\Gamma}
. Now we may readily calculate

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{g}_{n}\right\|_{L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r))}=\sum_{q=1}^{n}\frac{2^{q(1+|R_{\Gamma}|/2)}}{q}2^{-q|R_{\Gamma}|}\mathscr{H}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}(\mathcal{C})=\mathscr{H}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}(\mathcal{C})\sum_{q=1}^{n}\frac{2^{q(1-|R_{\Gamma}|/2)}}{q}\to\infty\;\text{as}\;n\to\infty,

(2.16)

since
|R_{\Gamma}|\leqslant 2
. On the other hand, for any
n\geqslant 1
we have

\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{X^{s}}^{2}\asymp\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|\xi|^{2}|G_{n}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\sum_{q=1}^{n}\int_{2^{-q}\mathcal{C}}|\omega|^{2}\left(\frac{2^{q(1+|R_{\Gamma}|/2)}}{q}\right)^{2}d\omega\\
\asymp\mathscr{H}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}(\mathcal{C})\sum_{q=1}^{n}2^{-2q}2^{-q|R_{\Gamma}|}\left(\frac{2^{q(1+|R_{\Gamma}|/2)}}{q}\right)^{2}=\mathscr{H}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}(\mathcal{C})\sum_{q=1}^{n}\frac{1}{q^{2}}.

(2.17)

Therefore,
\sup_{n\geqslant 1}\left\|g_{n}\right\|_{X^{s}}<\infty
and by (
2.14
) and (
2.16
), we arrive at a contradiction. Thus,
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
cannot be complete for
|R_{\Gamma}|\leqslant 2
.
∎

We now proceed to study the space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
in the second case of Lemma
2.2
. We begin by stating a preliminary result.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\Gamma
be defined as in (
1.43
) and
r
be defined as in (
A.13
). Suppose
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
,
1\in R_{\Gamma}
, and
|R_{\Gamma}|\geqslant 2
. The following hold.

(1)

For
\upmu:\hat{\Gamma}\to\mathbb{R}
defined in (
2.4
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\frac{1}{\upmu^{2}(\xi)}\;d\xi<\infty.

(2.18)

(2)

For
f\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|\hat{f}(\xi)|d\xi+\left(\int_{\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s}|\hat{f}(\xi)|\right)^{1/2}\lesssim_{d,s}\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.19)

In particular, if
s>d/2
then

\displaystyle\|\hat{f}\|_{L^{1}(\hat{\Gamma})}\lesssim_{d,s}\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.20)

Proof.

For the first item, we note that by Lemma
A.6
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\frac{1}{\upmu^{2}(\xi)}\;d\xi\asymp\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4}}\;d\xi=\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\frac{|\xi_{R_{\hat{\Gamma}}}|^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi_{R_{\hat{\Gamma}}}|^{4}}\;d\xi=\int_{B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Gamma}}|}}(0,r)}\frac{|\omega|^{2}}{\omega_{1}^{2}+|\omega|^{4}}\;d\omega,

(2.21)

where in the last equality we used the isometric measure-preserving group isomorphism
\uppi_{\hat{\Gamma}}
between
B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)
and
B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Gamma}}|}}(0,r)
. The latter integral can readily be verified to be finite; see for instance, Proposition 5.2 of
[
18
]
.

For the second item, we note that by (
2.2
), we have
