\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}^{2}\asymp\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\upmu^{2}(\xi)|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi+\int_{\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\langle\xi\rangle^{2s}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi.

(2.22)

Then by Cauchy-Schwarz, (
2.18
), and (
2.22
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|\hat{f}(\xi)|\;d\xi\leqslant\left(\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\frac{1}{\upmu^{2}(\xi)}\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}\upmu^{2}(\xi)|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.23)

Then (
2.19
) follows immediately from (
2.22
) and (
2.23
).

∎

The next theorem records the fundamental completeness and embedding properties of the space
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
in the first and second cases considered in Lemma
2.2
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Suppose
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
. Let
\Gamma
be defined as in (
1.43
) and let
R_{\Gamma}
be defined as in (
A.2
). Assume
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing,R_{\Gamma}=\{1\}
, or
1\in R_{\Gamma}
and
|R_{\Gamma}|\geqslant 2
. Then the following hold.

(1)

X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is a Hilbert space.

(2)

The Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
, as defined in Definition
A.7
, is dense in
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
.

(3)

If
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
s<t
, then we have the continuous inclusion
X^{t}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow X^{s}(\Gamma\;\mathbb{F})
.

(4)

We have the continuous inclusion
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\hookrightarrow X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
.

(5)

If
s\geqslant 1
, then there exists a constant
c>0
depending on
d,s
, and in the toroidal cases on
L_{i}
, such that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla f\right\|_{H^{s-1}}\leqslant c\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.24)

In particular, this implies that the map
\nabla:X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})\to H^{s-1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F}^{d})
is continuous.

Proof.

In the case when
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
or
R_{\Gamma}=\{1\}
, by the first item of Lemma
2.2
, all five items follow from standard Sobolev theory as
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is the standard Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
.

Next suppose that
1\in R_{\Gamma}
and
|R_{\Gamma}|\geqslant 2
. If a sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is Cauchy, then there exists
F\in L^{2}_{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
for which
\hat{f}_{n}\to F
in
L^{2}_{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
as
n\to\infty
. The second item of Lemma
2.4
guarantees that
F\subseteq L^{1}(B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r);\mathbb{C})+L^{2}(\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r);\mathbb{C})
, therefore
f:=\check{F}\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is well-defined and it is easy to verify that
f
is real-valued in the case when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, as realness is preserved in the limit. This implies
f_{n}\to f
in
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
as
n\to\infty
, and it follows then that
X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F})
is complete. This proves the first item. Following the arguments of Theorem 5.6 of
[
18
]
, the first item in turn implies the other fundamental properties listed in the second to fifth items.
∎

We conclude this subsection by summarizing some additional properties of the space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, where
\Sigma
is defined as in (
1.1
).

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
\mathbb{R}\ni s\geqslant 0
and let
\Sigma
be defined as in (
1.1
). Assume
R_{\Sigma}=\varnothing,R_{\Sigma}=\{1\}
, or
1\in R_{\Sigma}
and
|R_{\Sigma}|\geqslant 2
. Then following hold.

(1)

(Low-high frequency decomposition) For every
f\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
and
t>0
, we can write
f=f_{l,t}+f_{h,t}
, where
f_{l,t}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,t)}\mathscr{F}[f]]\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
and
f_{h,t}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{\Gamma}\setminus B(0,t)}\mathscr{F}[f]]\in H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C})
. Furthermore, we have the estimates

\displaystyle\left\|f_{l,t}\right\|_{C^{k}_{b}}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leqslant k}\left\|\partial^{\alpha}f_{l,R}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\left\|f_{l,R}\right\|_{X^{s}}\;\text{and}\;\left\|f_{h,t}\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\left\|f_{h,t}\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.25)

(2)

(Supercritical specialized Sobolev times standard Sobolev is standard Sobolev) If
s>d/2
, then for any
f\in X^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{F}),g\in H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
we have
fg\in H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
, and there exists a constant
c=c(d,s)>0
for which

\displaystyle\left\|fg\right\|_{H^{s}}\leqslant c\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{s}}\;\text{for all }f\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})\;\text{and}\;g\in H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F}).

(2.26)

(3)

(
e_{1}
-derivatives of specialized Sobolev are
\dot{H}^{-1}
bounded) If
s\geqslant 1
, then there exists a constant
c=c(d,s)>0
such that

\displaystyle[\partial_{1}\eta]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\leqslant c\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}\;\text{for all }f\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F}).

(2.27)

This implies that the map
\partial_{1}:X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})\to\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})\cap H^{s-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
is continuous and injective.

Proof.

If
R_{\Sigma}=\varnothing
or
R_{\Sigma}=\{1\}
, then by the first item of Lemma
2.2
,
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is the standard Sobolev space
H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. All three items then follow from standard results on
H^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. Suppose
1\in R_{\Sigma}
and
|R_{\Sigma}|\geqslant 2
. We note that by Lemma
A.6
and Remark
3.2
, the calculations performed over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in
[
18
]
are also valid over the low frequencies belonging to general
\hat{\Gamma}
, therefore all three items follow from minimal modifications of Theorem 5.5, the last item of Theorem 5.6, and Theorem 5.12 of
[
18
]
.
∎

2.2.
The anisotropic space
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
as an algebra

Let
\Sigma
be defined as in (
1.1
). In this subsection we prove that
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
is an algebra for
s>\frac{d}{2}
. To prove this we will first adapt the anisotropic Littlewood-Paley techniques used in
[
11
]
to prove that
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an algebra. This special case turns out to be sufficient for deducing the result in the general case when
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is replaced by
\Sigma
and
\mathbb{C}
is replaced by
\mathbb{R}
.

First, recall that the multiplier
\upmu
defined in (
2.4
) satisfies
1/\upmu\in L^{2}(B(0,1))
by the first item of Lemma
2.4
. Next, we consider the functional
I:(L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}\to[0,\infty]
defined via

\displaystyle I[F,G,H]=\int_{B(0,1)^{2}}\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta,

(2.28)

where
L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
denote the non-negative measurable functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Our goal is to use the same formula (
2.28
) to define a trilinear functional over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}))^{3}
, but for now we only define
I
over non-negative measurable functions so that
I
is clearly well-defined.

The next lemma shows that
I
induces a bounded trilinear functional over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}))^{3}
into
\mathbb{C}
as long as
I
is bounded over
(L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}
into
\mathbb{R}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Suppose there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle I[F,G,H]\leqslant c\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}\;\text{for all}\;F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]),

(2.29)

where
I
is the functional defined in (
2.28
). Then
I
induces a bounded trilinear map defined over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}))^{3}
into
\mathbb{C}
via the same formula in (
2.28
). Furthermore, there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle|I[F,G,H]|\leqslant C\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}\;\text{for all}\;F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}).

(2.30)

Proof.

We first consider the case when
F,G,H\in(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}))^{3}
. Then by decomposing
F=F_{0}-F_{1}
where
F_{0}=F^{+}
and
F_{1}=F^{-}
are the positive and negative parts of
F
, and similarly for
G,H
, we have

\displaystyle FGH=\sum_{j,k,l\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{j+k+l}F_{j}G_{k}H_{l}.

(2.31)

By linearity,

\displaystyle I[F,G,H]=\sum_{j,k,l\in\{0,1\}}(-1)^{j+k+l}I[F_{j},G_{k},H_{l}].

(2.32)

By assumption,
|I[F_{j},G_{k},H_{l}]|\leqslant c\left\|F_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G_{k}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H_{l}\right\|_{L^{2}}
for all
j,k,l\in\{0,1\}
, thus by (
2.32
)
I
is bounded over
F,G,H\in(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}))^{3}
and
|I[F,G,H]|\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}
for all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
.

In the general case when
F,G,H\in(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}))^{3}
, we write
F=F_{0}+iF_{1}
where
F_{0},F_{1}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
, and similarly for
G,H
. Then

\displaystyle FGH=\sum_{j,k,l\in\{0,1\}}(i)^{j+k+l}F_{j}G_{k}H_{l},

(2.33)

and by linearity again we have

\displaystyle I[F,G,H]=\sum_{j,k,l\in\{0,1\}}(i)^{j+k+l}I[F_{j},G_{k},H_{l}].

(2.34)

By the first case and following the same line of reasoning,
I
is bounded over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}))^{3}
into
\mathbb{C}
and there exists a constant
C>0
for which (
2.30
) holds.
∎

Next, we claim that supercritical specialized Sobolev space is an algebra if and only if the functional defined via (
2.28
) is bounded over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}
into
[0,\infty]
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Assume
s>d/2
. Then
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an algebra if and only if for the mapping
I
defined via (
2.28
), there exists a constant
c>0
such that for all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
we have the estimate
I[F,G,H]\leqslant c\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}
.

Proof.

Assume first that
I
is bounded over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}
. By Lemma
2.7
,
I
induces a bounded trilinear functional over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C}))^{3}
into
\mathbb{C}
via the same formula (
2.28
), and there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle|I[F,G,H]|\leqslant C\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}},

(2.35)

for all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
.

Now suppose that
f,g\in X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
. By the second item in Theorem
2.6
, we may write
f=f_{0}+f_{1}
where
f_{0}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}\mathscr{F}[f]]\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
and
f_{1}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,1)}\mathscr{F}[f]]\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, and similarly for
g
. Then we have the decomposition

\displaystyle fg=f_{0}g_{0}+f_{0}g_{1}+f_{1}g_{0}+f_{1}g_{1}.

(2.36)

If
1\leqslant i+j
, by the fact that we are in the supercritical regime
s>d/2
,
f_{1},g_{1}\in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, (
2.25
), and (
2.26
) we have
f_{i}g_{j}\in H^{s}
with the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|f_{i}g_{j}\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}\left\|g\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.37)

Thus, it remains to use the boundedness of
I
to understand the product
f_{0}g_{0}
. Note that by the first item of Theorem
2.6
, we have the inclusions
\hat{f}_{0},\hat{g}_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, and hence Young’s inequality implies that
\hat{f}_{0}\ast\hat{g}_{0}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
; also,
\supp(\hat{f}_{0}\ast\hat{g}_{0})\subseteq B(0,2)
.

Now let
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
. Since
\hat{f}_{0}
and
\hat{g}_{0}
are supported in
B(0,1)
and
\upmu
is locally bounded, we may employ Tonelli’s theorem and a change of variables to see that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\varphi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\upmu(\xi+\eta)\hat{f_{0}}(\xi)\hat{g_{0}}(\eta)\varphi(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta\\
=\int_{B(0,1)^{2}}\upmu(\xi+\eta)\hat{f_{0}}(\xi)\hat{g_{0}}(\eta)\varphi(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta=I[\upmu\hat{f_{0}},\upmu\hat{g_{0}},\varphi].

(2.38)

Therefore, by (
2.35
) we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\varphi\Big|\leqslant C\lVert\upmu\hat{f_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\upmu\hat{g_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}},

(2.39)

but by the density of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
, the left side of this expression extends to define a bounded linear functional on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
obeying the same estimate.
Upon invoking Riesz’s representation theorem, we deduce that
\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
with

\displaystyle\lVert\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\lVert\upmu\hat{f_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\upmu\hat{g_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leqslant C\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}\left\|g\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.40)

As
\mathrm{supp}(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\subseteq B(0,2)
, we have that
\left\|\langle\cdot\rangle^{s-1}\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant 5^{\frac{s-1}{2}}\left\|\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\right\|_{L^{2}}
. Thus, using (
2.37
), (
2.40
), and the fourth item of Theorem
2.6
we find that

\left\|fg\right\|_{X^{s}}\leqslant\sum_{0\leqslant i+j\leqslant 2}\left\|f_{i}g_{j}\right\|_{X^{s}}=\lVert\upmu\langle\cdot\rangle^{s-1}(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\rVert_{L^{2}}+\sum_{1\leqslant i+j\leqslant 2}\left\|f_{i}g_{j}\right\|_{X^{s}}\\
\lesssim\lVert\upmu(\hat{f_{0}}\ast\hat{g_{0}})\rVert_{L^{2}}+\sum_{1\leqslant i+j\leqslant 2}\left\|f_{i}g_{j}\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\left\|f\right\|_{X^{s}}\left\|g\right\|_{X^{s}}.

(2.41)

Thus,
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an algebra.

Conversely, assume that
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an algebra. Let
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
and observe first that
I[F,G,H]=I[F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)},G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)},H]
and then that
\mathscr{F}^{-1}[F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu],\mathscr{F}^{-1}[G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu]\in X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
. Therefore, by Cauchy-Schwarz and the boundedness of products in
X^{s}
we have

I[F,G,H]=I[F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)},G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)},H]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\upmu\big((F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu)\ast(G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu)\big)H\\
\leqslant\lVert\upmu((F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu)\ast(G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu))\rVert_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}=\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}[F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu]\mathscr{F}^{-1}[G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu]\rVert_{X^{s}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}\\
\lesssim\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}[F\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu]\rVert_{X^{s}}\lVert\mathscr{F}^{-1}[G\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}/\upmu]\rVert_{X^{s}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.42)

Hence,
I
is bounded over
(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}
and the proof is complete.
∎

Thus, by Lemma
2.7
and Proposition
2.8
, to prove that
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
is an algebra it remains to show that the functional
I
defined over
(L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}
via (
2.28
) satisfies (
2.29
). For the rest of this subsection we assume that
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
, and we now estimate the
L^{2}
-boundedness of the operator
I
.

First, we introduce a decomposition of the frequency space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Definition 2.9

(Squared frequency space and functional decomposition)
.

We write
I
as the sum of two operators
I=I_{0}+I_{1}
, where each
I_{i}
is accounting for the contribution from a special portion of squared frequency space.

(1)

We identify the following ‘good’ and ‘bad’ sets. First we define the ‘good’ set
E_{0}
via

\displaystyle E_{0}=\{{(\xi,\eta)\in B(0,1)^{2}\;:\;|\xi|+|\eta|\leqslant 3||\xi|-|\eta||}\}.

(2.43)

Next we define the ‘bad’ set
E_{1}
via

\displaystyle E_{1}=\{{(\xi,\eta)\in B(0,1)^{2}\;:\;|\xi|+|\eta|>3||\xi|-|\eta||}\}.

(2.44)

Note that
B(0,1)^{2}=E_{0}\cup E_{1}
.

(2)

For
i\in\{0,1\}
, we define the functionals
I_{i}:(L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty]))^{3}\to[0,\infty]
via

\displaystyle I_{i}[F,G,H]=\int_{E_{i}}\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta.

(2.45)

Clearly,
I_{i}
is well-defined, and we have the identity
I=I_{0}+I_{1}
.

Next, we analyze the set
E_{1}
as defined in (
2.44
).

Lemma 2.10
.

The inclusion
(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}
is equivalent to the bounds
\frac{1}{2}|\eta|<|\xi|<2|\eta|
, and for
(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}
we have the estimate
|\xi+\eta|<3|\xi|
.

Proof.

The second bound follows from the equivalence and the triangle inequality, so it suffices to prove the equivalence. For this we note that

\displaystyle(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}\Longleftrightarrow|\xi|+|\eta|>3||\xi|-|\eta||\Longleftrightarrow|\xi|^{2}-\frac{5}{2}|\xi||\eta|+|\eta|^{2}<0\Longleftrightarrow\frac{1}{2}|\eta|<|\xi|<2|\eta|.

(2.46)

∎

By Lemma
2.10
, one can get a good geometric understanding of
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
by examining the plot in
(|\xi|,|\eta|)
space given in Figure
3
.

Figure 3.
A schematic breakdown of the sets
E_{0}
and
E_{1}

The region
E_{0}
is dubbed the good region because of the following lemma.

Lemma 2.11

(Multiplier subadditivity in
E_{0}
)
.

If
(\xi,\eta)\in E_{0}
, then
\upmu(\xi+\eta)\leqslant 3(\upmu(\xi)+\upmu(\eta))
.

Proof.

If
|\xi|+|\eta|\leqslant 3||\xi|-|\eta||
, then the reverse triangle inequality
|\xi+\eta|\geqslant||\xi|-|\eta||
allows us to deduce

\displaystyle\upmu(\xi+\eta)=\frac{|\xi_{1}+\eta_{1}|}{|\xi+\eta|}+|\xi+\eta|\leqslant\frac{|\xi_{1}|+|\eta_{1}|}{||\xi|-|\eta||}+|\xi|+|\eta|\leqslant 3\frac{|\xi_{1}|+|\eta_{1}|}{|\xi|+|\eta|}+|\xi|+|\eta|\leqslant 3(\upmu(\xi)+\upmu(\eta)).

(2.47)

∎

This gives us the boundedness of
I_{0}
.

Proposition 2.12
.

For all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty))
we have the estimate

\displaystyle I_{0}[F,G,H]\leqslant 6\left\|1/\upmu\right\|_{L^{2}(B(0,1))}\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.48)

Proof.

By applying Lemma
2.11
, Tonelli’s theorem, and Cauchy-Schwarz, we find

I_{0}[F,G,H]\leqslant 3\int_{E_{0}}\frac{1}{\upmu(\xi)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta+3\int_{E_{0}}\frac{1}{\upmu(\eta)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta\\
\leqslant 3\int_{B(0,1)^{2}}\frac{1}{\upmu(\xi)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta+3\int_{B(0,1)^{2}}\frac{1}{\upmu(\eta)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta\\
\leqslant 3\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}\Big(\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{1}{\upmu(\xi)}F(\xi)\;d\xi+\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\int_{B(0,1)}\frac{1}{\upmu(\eta)}G(\eta)\;d\eta\Big)\\
\leqslant 6\left\|1/\upmu\right\|_{L^{2}(B(0,1))}\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.49)

∎

We now further decompose the set
E_{1}
and the functional
I_{1}
, as defined in (
2.44
) and (
2.45
).

Definition 2.13
.

We make the following definitions for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

We define the set

\displaystyle E_{m,n}=\{{(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}\;:\;2^{-m-1}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant 2^{-m}\;\;\text{and}\;\;2^{-n+1}\leqslant|\xi+\eta|\leqslant 2^{-n+2}}\}.

(2.50)

(2)

We define the functional
I_{m,n}:(L^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}))^{3}\to[0,\infty]
via

\displaystyle I_{m,n}[F,G,H]=\int_{E_{m,n}}\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}F(\xi)G(\eta)H(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta.

(2.51)

The dyadic decomposition of
E_{1}
gives us the following.

Lemma 2.14
.

The following hold.

(1)

We have the equalities

\displaystyle\bigcup_{m=0}^{\infty}\bigcup_{n=m}^{\infty}E_{m,n}=E_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}I_{m,n}=I_{1}.

(2.52)

(2)

Let
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
. Then

\displaystyle I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant I_{1}[F_{m},G_{m},H_{n}],

(2.53)

for the functions

\displaystyle F_{m}=F\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-m-1}A},\quad G_{m}=G\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-m-1}A},\quad\text{and}\quad H_{n}=H\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-n}A},

(2.54)

where
A
is the annulus
A=\{{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\;:\;1/2\leqslant|x|\leqslant 4}\}
.

(3)

We have
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-almost everywhere the inequality

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-m-1}A}\leqslant 5\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,2)}

(2.55)

Proof.

For the first item we note that the second estimate of Lemma
2.10
implies that if
n<m
then
E_{m,n}=\varnothing
, and hence
I_{m,n}=0
. The second item follows from the first item of Lemma
2.10
and nonnegativity. To prove the third item, we note that if
x\in B(0,2)\setminus\{{0}\}
, there exists a unique
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|x|\in 2^{-n}[1,2)
. It is clear that
\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-m-1}A}(x)=0
whenever
m\geqslant 0
and
|m-n|>2
. The inequality now follows.
∎

Using the preceding lemma we arrive at the following proposition.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
. The following hold:

(1)

If
d\geqslant 4
, there exists a constant
C_{d}>0
such that for all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
we have the estimate
I_{1}[F,G,H]\leqslant C_{d}\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}
.

(2)

If
d\in\{{2,3}\}
, there exists a constant
C_{1,d}>0
such that for all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty])
we have the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=2m+1}^{\infty}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant C_{1,d}\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.56)

Proof.

Let
m,n,d\in\mathbb{N}
with
2\leqslant d
and
m\leqslant n
. According to the second item of Lemma
2.14
, we have

\displaystyle I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant I_{1}[F_{m},G_{m},H_{n}]=\int_{E_{1}}\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}F_{m}(\xi)G_{m}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta.

(2.57)

For
(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}
, the right hand integrand vanishes except possibly when the following inequalities hold:
2^{-m-2}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant 2^{-m+1}
,
2^{-m-2}\leqslant|\eta|\leqslant 2^{-m+1}
, and
2^{-n-1}\leqslant|\xi+\eta|\leqslant 2^{-n+2}
. This provides the elementary estimate

\displaystyle\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}\leqslant\frac{1+|\xi+\eta|}{|\xi||\eta|}\leqslant 2^{2m+4}(1+2^{-n+2})\lesssim 2^{2m},

(2.58)

and hence

I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim 2^{2m}\int_{E_{1}}F_{m}(\xi)G_{m}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta.

(2.59)

Now, for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
let
Q_{\ell}
denote the closed cube centered at
2^{-n}\ell
of side length
2^{-n}
and
\tilde{Q}_{\ell}
denote the closed cube centered at
-2^{-n}\ell
of side length
9\cdot 2^{-n}
. By construction, almost everywhere we have that

1=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}\quad\text{and}\quad 9^{d}=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}},

(2.60)

and we may compute

\max_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}\rVert_{L^{2}}=\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}=2^{-nd/2}.

(2.61)

Notice that if
(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}
is such that the integrand on the right hand side of (
2.59
) is nonzero and
\xi\in Q_{\ell}
, then

\displaystyle|\eta+2^{-n}\ell|_{\infty}\leqslant|\eta+\xi|+|-\xi+2^{-n}\ell|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{-n+2}+2^{-n-1}=\frac{9}{2}\cdot 2^{-n},

(2.62)

which in particular implies that
\eta\in\tilde{Q}_{\ell}
. Hence,

\int_{E_{1}}F_{m}(\xi)G_{m}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta\leqslant\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)^{2}}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}(\xi)G_{m}(\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta.

(2.63)

By Tonelli’s theorem, repeated applications of Cauchy-Schwarz, and the identities in (
2.60
) and (
2.61
) we then find that

\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)^{2}}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}(\xi)G_{m}(\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta\\
=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}(\xi)\int_{B(0,1)}G_{m}(\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\;d\eta\;d\xi\\
\leqslant\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\int_{B(0,1)}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}(\xi)\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\left\|H_{n}\right\|_{L^{2}}\;d\xi\leqslant\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{0}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\leqslant 2^{-nd/2}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|F_{m}(\xi)|^{2}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{Q_{\ell}}(\xi)\;d\xi\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|G_{m}(\eta)|^{2}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{Q}_{\ell}}(\eta)\;d\eta\Big)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim 2^{-nd/2}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.64)

Next we combine equations (
2.59
), (
2.63
), and (
2.64
) to deduce that

I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim 2^{2m-nd/2}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.65)

Now we break to cases based on the dimension. First, if
d\geqslant 4
, then there is no issue in summing over the set
\{{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\;:\;m\leqslant n}\}
. Indeed, we use Cauchy-Schwarz along with the third item of Lemma
2.14
to find

\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}2^{2m-nd/2}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\leqslant\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}2^{2m}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\Big(\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}2^{-nd}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|H(\zeta)|^{2}\sum_{n=m}^{\infty}\mathbbm{1}_{2^{-n}A}(\zeta)\;d\zeta\Big)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}2^{(2-d/2)m}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.66)

This completes the proof of the first item.

For the second item, we let
d\in\{{2,3}\}
in equation (
2.65
) and sum over the set
\{{(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\;:\;2m+1\leqslant n}\}
. Arguing as above, we arrive at the bound

\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=2m+1}^{\infty}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}2^{(2-d)m}\Big(\sum_{n=2m+1}^{\infty}2^{-d(n-2m)}\Big)^{1/2}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\lesssim\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.67)

This completes the proof of the second item.
∎

It remains to study the functional
\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=m}^{2m}I_{m,n}
in the case
d\in\{{2,3}\}
. We do this in the subsequent proposition.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
d\in\{{2,3}\}
. There exists a constant
C_{2,d}>0
such that for all
F,G,H\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty))
we may estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=m}^{2m}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant C_{2,d}\left\|F\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|G\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|H\right\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.68)

Proof.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m\leqslant n\leqslant 2m
. We begin by estimating the right hand side of the following inequality of Lemma
2.14
,
I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant I_{1}[F_{m},G_{m},H_{n}]
, by decomposing into a rectangular grid. To this end we define the following family of rectangles indexed by
p\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
, and
\alpha\in[1,\infty)
, via

\displaystyle R_{p,\pi}(\alpha)=2^{-2m}[-\alpha/2+p,\alpha/2+p]\times 2^{-n}\prod_{\nu=1}^{d-1}[-\alpha/2+\pi_{\nu},\alpha/2+\pi_{\nu}].

(2.69)

Suppose that
(\xi,\eta)\in E_{1}
are such that the integrand of
I_{1}[F_{m},G_{m},H_{n}]
is nonzero and that additionally
\xi\in R_{p,\pi}(1)
and
\eta\in R_{q,\sigma}(1)
for some
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\pi,\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
. Then we obtain the inequalities

\displaystyle 2^{-n}(\pi_{\nu}+\sigma_{\nu}-1/2)\leqslant\xi_{\nu+1}+\eta_{\nu+1}\leqslant 2^{-n}(\pi_{\nu}+\sigma_{\nu}+1/2)\text{ for all }1\leqslant\nu\leqslant d-1,

(2.70)

and upon pairing these with the bound
|\xi+\eta|\leqslant 2^{-n+2}
we deduce that

\displaystyle|\pi_{\nu}+\sigma_{\nu}|\leqslant|\pi_{\nu}+\sigma_{\nu}-2^{n}(\xi_{\nu+1}+\eta_{\nu+1})|+2^{n}|\xi_{\nu+1}+\eta_{\nu+1}|\leqslant 9/2\text{ for all }1\leqslant\nu\leqslant d-1.

(2.71)

In particular, this implies that
\eta\in R_{q,-\pi}(9)
, which when combined with the inclusion
\xi\in R_{p,\pi}(1)
further implies that
\xi+\eta\in R_{p+q,0}(10)
. Therefore, we may estimate

I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\int_{E_{1}}\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}(\xi)G_{m}(\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,-\pi}(9)}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}(\xi+\eta)\;d\xi\;d\eta.

(2.72)

For fixed
p
,
q
, and
\pi
, we now want to estimate the factor
\upmu(\xi+\eta)/\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)
appearing in the integrand in (
2.72
). First we note that if
\xi\in R_{p,\pi}(1)\cap 2^{-m-1}A
, then
|2^{2m}\xi_{1}-p|\leqslant 1/2
, which implies that
2^{2m}|\xi_{1}|\geqslant\max\{{0,|p|-1/2}\}
and hence

\upmu(\xi)=\frac{|\xi_{1}|}{|\xi|}+|\xi|\geqslant 2^{-m-1}(2^{2m}|\xi_{1}|+1/2)\gtrsim 2^{-m}\max\{{1,|p|}\}.

(2.73)

Similarly, for
\eta\in R_{q,-\pi}(9)\cap 2^{-m-1}A
, we have the bound

\upmu(\eta)\geqslant 2^{-m-1}(\max\{|q|-9/2,0\}+1/2)\gtrsim 2^{-m}\max\{{1,|q|}\}.

(2.74)

Finally, for
\xi+\eta\in R_{p+q,0}(10)\cap 2^{-n}A
, we estimate
\upmu(\xi+\eta)
from above, recalling that
m\leqslant n
:

\upmu(\xi+\eta)\leqslant 2^{n+1}|\xi_{1}+\eta_{1}|+2^{-n+2}=2^{n-2m+1}(2^{2m}|\xi_{1}+\eta_{1}|+2^{2(m-n)+1})\\
\leqslant 2^{n-2m+1}(|p+q|+5+2)\lesssim 2^{n-2m}(\max\{{1,|p|}\}+\max\{{1,|q|}\}).

(2.75)

Upon piecing equations (
2.73
), (
2.74
), and (
2.75
) all together, we obtain the bound

\frac{\upmu(\xi+\eta)}{\upmu(\xi)\upmu(\eta)}\lesssim 2^{n}\Big(\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}+\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\Big)

(2.76)

for
\xi\in R_{p,\pi}(1)\cap 2^{-m-1}A
and
\eta\in R_{q,-\pi}(9)\cap 2^{-m-1}A
.

Next, we combine (
2.72
) and (
2.76
) and then use Tonelli’s theorem and Cauchy-Schwarz to obtain

2^{-n}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\leqslant\\
\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}\int_{B(0,1)}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}(\xi)\int_{B(0,1)}G_{m}(\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,-\pi}(9)}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}(\xi+\eta)\;d\eta\;d\xi\\
+\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\int_{B(0,1)}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}(\xi)\int_{B(0,1)}G_{m}(\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,-\pi}(9)}(\eta)H_{n}(\xi+\eta)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}(\xi+\eta)\;d\eta\;d\xi\\
\leqslant\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\Big(\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}+\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\Big)\int_{B(0,1)}F_{m}(\xi)\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}(\xi)\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\;d\xi\\
\leqslant\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\Big(\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}+\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\Big)\lVert\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,\-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
=2^{-m-n(d-1)/2}\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\Big(\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}+\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\Big)\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,\-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.77)

What remains, before we sum over
m
and
n
, is a few more applications of Cauchy-Schwarz, one for each sum above. First we handle the sum over
\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}
with

\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,\-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\leqslant\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|F_{m}(\xi)|^{2}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p,\pi}(1)}(\xi)\;d\xi\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|G_{m}(\eta)|^{2}\sum_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{q,-\pi}(9)}(\eta)\;d\eta\Big)^{1/2}\\
\lesssim\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{q,-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.78)

Next we consider the sums over
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
. First, we consider the one containing
1/\max\{{1,|p|}\}
,

\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{q,-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\lesssim\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{q,-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|p|}\}}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}R_{p+q,0}(10)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\leqslant\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}\Big(\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,p^{2}}\}}\Big)^{1/2}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert\lesssim\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.79)

Similarly, we consider the one containing
1/\max\{{1,|q|}\}
,

\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\lVert F_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{p,\pi}(1)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{q,-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\mathbbm{1}_{R_{p+q,0}(10)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\lesssim\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{\max\{{1,|q|}\}}\lVert G_{m}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{\pi\in\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}R_{q,-\pi}(9)}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.80)

Synthesizing equations (
2.77
), (
2.78
), (
2.79
), and (
2.80
) we find that

\displaystyle I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim 2^{-m+n(3-d)/2}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H_{n}\rVert_{L^{2}}.

(2.81)

Hence, we may sum over
m\leqslant n\leqslant 2m
to bound

\sum_{n=m}^{2m}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim 2^{-m}\Big(\sum_{n=m}^{2m}2^{n(3-d)}\Big)^{1/2}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\\
\leqslant 2^{-m}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\cdot\begin{cases}\sqrt{m+1}&\text{if}\;d=3\\
2^{m+1/2}&\text{if}\;d=2\end{cases}\lesssim\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}},

(2.82)

and then finally sum over
m\geqslant 0
to see that

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=m}^{2m}I_{m,n}[F,G,H]\lesssim\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\lVert F_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G_{m}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lesssim\lVert F\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert G\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert H\rVert_{L^{2}},

(2.83)

which is the stated estimate.
∎

By synthesizing the previous results, we immediately arrive at the following result.

Theorem 2.17

(Boundedness of
I
)
.

The functional
I:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[0,\infty))^{3}\to[0,\infty]
defined in (
2.28
) is bounded and satisfies the estimate (
2.30
).

Proof.

We simply combine Lemma
2.14
and Propositions
2.12
,
2.15
,
2.16
, and
2.8
.
∎

With Theorem
2.17
in hand, we are ready to prove Theorem
1.1
as a corollary.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

By Lemma
2.7
, Proposition
2.8
, and
Theorem
2.17
, the anisotropic space
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{k};\mathbb{F})
is an algebra for
s>k/2
for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
. Let
\Sigma
be defined as in (
1.1
) and let
d=\dim\Sigma
. By the first item of Lemma
2.2
, it suffices show that
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
is an algebra for
s>d/2
for the case when
1\in R_{\Sigma}
and
|R_{\Sigma}|\geqslant 2
, as the anisotropic space coincides with the standard Sobolev space otherwise. To show this we first argue similarly as in Proposition
2.8
to show that
X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
is an algebra if and only if for any
F,G\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
, the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|F_{0}G_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\left\|F_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma)}\left\|G_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma)}

(2.84)

holds for
F_{0}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}}(0,r)}\mathscr{F}[F]],G_{0}=\mathscr{F}^{-1}[\mathbbm{1}_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}}(0,r)}\mathscr{F}[G]]
.

Let
\Sigma_{\ast},\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}
be the reordered Cartesian products of
\Sigma
defined via (
A.10
), and
\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma},\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Sigma}}
be the canonical reordering maps defined by (
A.10
). We first note that since
F_{0},G_{0}\in X^{s}(\Sigma;\mathbb{F})
, by Lemma
2.1
the functions
F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}=F_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}^{-1}\cdot),G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}=G_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{\Sigma}^{-1}\cdot)
belong to
X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast};\mathbb{F})
with
\left\|F_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma)}=\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})},\left\|G_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma)}=\left\|G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}
, and by (
A.29
) we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}^{+}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\cdot)=\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma}[F_{0}](\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1}\cdot),\;\mathscr{F}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}^{+}[G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\cdot)=\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma}[G_{0}](\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1}\cdot).

(2.85)

We also have
\left\|F_{0}G_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma)}=\left\|\sigma_{\Sigma}F_{0}G_{0}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}=\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}
, where
\sigma_{\Sigma}
is defined via (
A.11
). So to establish (
2.84
) it suffices to establish the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}\lesssim\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}\left\|G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}.

(2.86)

By (
2.85
), we have
\supp\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}],\supp\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}]\subseteq B_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}(0,r)
. Since
\xi\in B_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}(0,r)\Longleftrightarrow\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}_{*,R}
, by Fubini’s theorem we then have

\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\omega,k)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}\sum_{m\in\prod_{i\in T_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}}L_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}}\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\omega-\eta,k-m)\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\eta,m)d\eta\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\omega-\eta,k)\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\eta,k)\;d\eta=\delta_{k0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\omega-\eta,0)\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\eta,0)\;d\eta,

(2.87)

where
\delta_{k0}
is the Kronecker delta. This shows that
\supp\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}]\subseteq B_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}(0,r)
. By Lemma
A.6
,
\uppi_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}
is an isometric measure-preserving group isomorphism between
B_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}(0,r)
and
B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}(0,r)
, and therefore

\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}^{2}=\int_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}(0,r)}\upmu^{2}(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\\
=\int_{B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}(0,r)}\upmu^{2}(\omega)\langle\omega\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\omega,0)|^{2}\;d\omega=\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\right\|^{2}_{X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|})}.

(2.88)

Next we note that since
F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\in X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast};\mathbb{F})
, we have the inclusions

F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|};\mathbb{C})\text{ and }\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\cdot,0)\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|};\mathbb{C}),

(2.89)

and we know that
\overline{F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)}=F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)
, and
\supp\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\cdot,0)\subseteq B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}(0,r)
. By Lemma
A.6
again, we have

\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\right\|_{X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|})}^{2}=\int_{B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|}}(0,r)}\upmu^{2}(\omega)\langle\omega\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\omega,0)|^{2}\;d\omega\\
=\int_{B_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}(0,r)}\upmu^{2}(\xi)\langle\xi\rangle^{2(s-1)}|\mathscr{F}^{+}_{\Sigma_{\ast}}[F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}](\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi=\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}\right\|_{X^{s}(\Sigma_{\ast})}^{2}.

(2.90)

This shows that
F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\in X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|};\mathbb{F})
, and similarly we can conclude that
G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\in X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|};\mathbb{F})
. Since
s>d/2\geqslant|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|/2
,
X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Sigma_{\ast}}|};\mathbb{F})
is an algebra, and therefore we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\right\|_{X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|})}\lesssim\left\|F_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\right\|_{X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|})}\left\|G_{0,\Sigma_{\ast}}(\cdot,0)\right\|_{X^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Sigma}_{\ast}}|})}.

(2.91)

Combining (
2.90
) and (
2.91
) then gives us (
2.86
) and in turn (
2.84
). The desired conclusion then follows.
∎

3.
Linear analysis

In this section we record the linear analysis associated to the flattened system (
1.24
). First, we introduce the definition of the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm.

Definition 3.1

(The
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm)
.

For
s\geqslant 0
, in the case when
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
, for
f\in H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
we define the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm of
f
via

\displaystyle[f]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}=\begin{cases}0,\;\text{if}\;\hat{f}(0)=0\\
\infty,\;\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3.1)

In the remaining cases, we define the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm of
f
via

\displaystyle[f]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}=\left(\int_{B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Gamma}}|}}(0,r)}|\omega|^{-2}|\hat{f}(\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1}l_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}\omega)|^{2}\;d\omega\right)^{1/2},

(3.2)

where
\Gamma_{\ast}
is defined in (
A.8
),

\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{\ast}}:\Gamma\to\Gamma_{\ast}
is defined in (
A.10
), and
l_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}:\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}\to\Gamma_{\ast}
is defined via
l_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}(\omega)=(\omega,0)
. For
f\in H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
, we write
f\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
to mean that
[f]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}<\infty
.

Remark 3.2
.

We note that our definition of the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm coincides with the standard definition of the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm for functions
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
. First, if a function
f
already belongs to
H^{s}(\Gamma;\mathbb{R})
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{\Gamma}}|\xi|^{-2}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\asymp\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|\xi|^{-2}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi.

(3.3)

Second, we note that the product measure on
\hat{\Gamma}
is a product of Lebesgue measures and counting measures, corresponding to the
\mathbb{R}
and
L_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
factors of
\hat{\Gamma}
. If
R_{\hat{\Gamma}}=\varnothing
, then the only low frequency mode is the 0 mode, and thus the integral in (
3.3
) takes values in
\{0,\infty\}
depending on the value of
\hat{f}(0)
. If
R_{\hat{\Gamma}}\neq\varnothing
, then we note that by Lemma
A.6
, using the natural isometric and measure-preserving identification between
B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)
and
B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\Gamma}|}}(0,r)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{\hat{\Gamma}}(0,r)}|\xi|^{-2}|\hat{f}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\asymp\int_{B_{\mathbb{R}^{|R_{\hat{\Gamma}}|}}(0,r)}|\omega|^{-2}|\hat{f}(\mathcal{P}_{\Gamma_{\ast}}^{-1}l_{\hat{\Gamma}_{\ast}}\omega)|^{2}\;d\omega.

(3.4)

3.1.
Asymptotics of the normal stress to solution map associated to (
1.37
)

In this subsection we record the asymptotics of some special functions associated to the normal stress to solution map associated to (
1.37
), which will play a crucial role in the construction of the free surface function
\eta
. In the case where
\hat{\Sigma}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, the precise asymptotics of these symbols were derived using ODE techniques developed in
[
18
]
. For the other possibilities of
\Sigma
considered in (
1.1
), these estimates will continue to hold as
\hat{\Sigma}
remains a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and the asymptotics remain the same upon subsampling.

First we record an auxiliary result.

Theorem 3.3

(Hilbert isomorphism for the
\gamma
-Stokes system)
.

Suppose
s\geqslant 0
. The map

\displaystyle\Phi_{\gamma}:{}_{0}H^{s+2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\to H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R}^{n})

(3.5)

defined via

\displaystyle\Phi_{\gamma}(u,p)=\left(\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u,\diverge u,S(p,u)e_{n}\right)

(3.6)

is a Hilbert isomorphism for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The case
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
follows from Theorem 2.6 in
[
18
]
, and the arguments therein can be adapted with minimal modification to handle the cases when
\Sigma\neq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.
∎

Next, we define the normal stress to velocity and pressure maps induced by
\Phi_{\gamma}
.

Definition 3.4

(Normal stress to velocity and pressure maps)
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
s\geqslant 0
. We define the normal stress to velocity and pressure maps to be the linear maps
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}:H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\to H^{s+2}\left(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}:H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\to H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
defined via

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}(\psi)=u,\;\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(\psi)=p,

(3.7)

where
(u,p)
is the unique solution to the
-\gamma
-Stokes system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(p,u)+\gamma\partial_{1}u=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge u=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
S(p,u)e_{n}=\psi e_{n},&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(3.8)

The existence and uniqueness of
(u,p)
and the boundedness of
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
are guaranteed by Theorem
3.3
.

Now we may record properties of the pseudodifferential operator associated to the normal stress to solution maps (
3.7
).

Theorem 3.5

(Symbols associated to the pseudodifferential operator and their asymptotics)
.

Suppose
s\geqslant 0
. The linear maps
\mathcal{U}_{\gamma},\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}
defined in (
3.7
) are well-defined and bounded. Moreover, there exists bounded and measurable functions
V:\hat{\Sigma}\times[0,b]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{n},Q:\hat{\Sigma}\times[0,b]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
m:\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
such that for all
\psi\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R})
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}(\psi)}(\xi,x_{n})=V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)\widehat{\psi}(\xi),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{P}_{\gamma}(\psi)}(\xi,x_{n})=Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)\widehat{\psi}(\xi),\quad m(\xi,\gamma)=V_{n}(\xi,b,\gamma).

(3.9)

Furthermore, the following hold.

(1)

V,Q,m
are continuous, with
V(0,x_{n},\gamma)=0,Q(0,x_{n},\gamma)=1
, and
m(0,\gamma)=0
.

(2)

\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)}=V(-\xi,x_{n},\gamma),\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)}=Q(-\xi,x_{n},\gamma),\overline{m(\xi,\gamma)}=m(-\xi,\gamma)
for all
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
.

(3)

For each
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
,
V(\xi,\cdot,\gamma),Q(\xi,\cdot,\gamma)
solve

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(-\partial_{n}^{2}+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\right)V^{\prime}+2\pi i\xi Q-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma V^{\prime}=0,&\text{in}\;(0,b)\\
\left(-\partial_{n}^{2}+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\right)V_{n}+\partial_{n}Q-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma V_{n}=0,&\text{in}\;(0,b)\\
2\pi i\xi\cdot V^{\prime}+\partial_{n}V_{n}=0,&\text{in}\;(0,b)\\
-\partial_{n}V^{\prime}-2\pi i\xi V_{n}=0,&\text{for}\;x_{n}=b\\
Q-2\partial_{n}V_{n}=1,&\text{for}\;x_{n}=b\\
V=0,&\text{for}\;x_{n}=0.\end{cases}

(3.10)

(4)

For
|\xi|\ll 1
, we have

V^{\prime}(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)=-\pi\xi(2x_{n}b-x_{n}^{2})+O(|\xi|^{2}),\quad V_{n}(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)=2\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}x_{n}^{2}\left(\frac{x_{n}}{3}-b\right)+O(|\xi|^{3}),\\
Q(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)=1+O(|\xi|^{2}),\quad m(\xi,\gamma)=-\frac{4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}b^{3}}{3}+O(|\xi|^{3}),\quad\text{as}\;|\xi|\to 0

(3.11)

where
F(\xi,x_{n})=O(|\xi|^{k})
as
|\xi|\to 0
means that

\displaystyle\limsup_{|\xi|\to 0}\sup_{0\leqslant x_{n}\leqslant b}\frac{|F(\xi,x_{n})|}{|\xi|^{k}}<\infty.

(3.12)

(5)

For each
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
c=c(\gamma,b)>0
and
R=R(\gamma,b)>0
such that for
x_{n}\in[0,b]
and
|\xi|>R
, we have the point-wise estimates

\displaystyle|V^{\prime}(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)|

\displaystyle\leqslant c\left(\frac{|\gamma|}{|\xi|^{2}}+(b-x_{n})\right)e^{-2\pi|\xi|(b-x_{n})}+ce^{-2\pi|\xi|b},

(3.13)

\displaystyle|V_{n}(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)|

\displaystyle\leqslant c\left(\frac{1}{|\xi|}+(b-x_{n})\right)e^{-2\pi|\xi|(b-x_{n})}+ce^{-2\pi|\xi|b},

(3.14)

\displaystyle|m(\xi,\gamma)+\frac{1}{4\pi|\xi|}|

\displaystyle\leqslant c\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}},

(3.15)

\displaystyle|Q(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)|

\displaystyle\leqslant ce^{-2\pi|\xi|(b-x_{n})}+ce^{-2\pi|\xi|b}.

(3.16)

(6)

For each
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
c=c(n,b,\gamma)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{|\xi|\leqslant 1,\xi\neq 0}\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{b}|V(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)|^{2}+|Q(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)-1|^{2}\;dx_{n}+|V(\xi,b,\gamma)|^{2}\right)\leqslant c

(3.17)

and

\displaystyle(1+|\xi|^{2})^{3/2}\int_{0}^{b}|V(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)|^{2}\;dx_{n}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{1/2}\int_{0}^{b}|Q(\xi,x_{n},\gamma)|^{2}\;dx_{n}+(1+|\xi|^{2})|V(\xi,b,\gamma)|^{2}\leqslant c,

(3.18)

for all
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

If
\hat{\Sigma}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, all six items follow from Theorem 4.5, Theorem 4.10, and Corollary 4.11 in
[
18
]
. In the other cases, we have
\hat{\Sigma}\subseteq\widehat{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. Therefore, the same conclusions will follow as we are sampling the frequencies on subgroups of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.
∎

We conclude this subsection by recording the properties of an auxiliary function defined in terms of
m
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and define

\displaystyle\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)=2\pi i\gamma\xi_{1}+(1+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2})\sigma\overline{m(\xi,-\gamma)}.

(3.19)

Then the following hold.

(1)

\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)=0
if and only if
\xi=0
, and
\overline{\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)}=\rho_{\gamma}(-\xi)
for all
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
.

(2)

For
\sigma>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,\gamma,\sigma,b)>0
such that for all
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle C^{-1}|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}\leqslant(\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+(1+|\xi|^{2})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\xi)\leqslant C|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}.

(3.20)

(3)

For
\sigma=0
and
n=2
, there exists a constant
C=C(\gamma,b)>0
such that for all
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle C^{-1}|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}\leqslant|\xi|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}(\xi)+(1+|\xi|^{2})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}(\xi)\leqslant C|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}.

(3.21)

Proof.

This follows from the proof for Lemma 6.1 in
[
18
]
and the asymptotics of
m(\xi,-\gamma)
on general
\hat{\Sigma}
recorded as the third and fourth items of Theorem
3.5
.
∎

3.2.
Compatibility conditions and the Hilbert isomorphism associated to the overdetermined
\gamma
-Stokes system

In this subsection we record the Hilbert isomorphism that completely characterize the solvability of the overdetermined system (
1.37
). First we introduce a pair of function spaces for the data tuple
(f,g,h,k)
that encodes the compatibility conditions associated to (
1.37
).

Definition 3.7
.

Let
s\geqslant 0
and
\Sigma
be defined as in (
1.1
). We say that a data tuple
(f,g,h,k)\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+3/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy the divergence-trace compatibility condition if

\displaystyle h-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}).

(3.22)

We define the Hilbert space
\mathcal{Y}^{s}
to be

\mathcal{Y}^{s}=\{(f,g,h,k)\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+3/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})\mid\\
(f,g,h,k)\;\text{satisfy the divergence-trace condition}\;\eqref{eq:divtrace}\},

(3.23)

with the associated norm defined via

\displaystyle\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}^{2}=\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}}^{2}+\left\|g\right\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\left\|k\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{H^{s+3/2}}^{2}+\left[h-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}.

(3.24)

Definition 3.8
.

Let
s\geqslant 0
. We say that the data tuple
(f,g,h,k)\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+3/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy the adjoint compatibility condition if for every
\psi\in H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{b}\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\;dx_{n}-\hat{k}(\xi)\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\hat{h}(\xi)=0,

(3.25)

where
V,Q
are defined in terms of
\psi
in (
3.10
). We define the closed subspace
\mathcal{Z}^{s}
of
\mathcal{Y}^{s}
to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{s}=\{(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}\mid(f,g,h,k)\;\text{satisfy}\;\eqref{eq: 2nd comp fourier}\;\forall\psi\in H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\}.

(3.26)

Now we are ready to record the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
s\geqslant 0,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{Z}^{s}
the Hilbert space defined in (
3.8
). Then the bounded linear operator
\Psi_{\gamma}:{}_{0}H^{s+2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathcal{Z}^{s}
given by

\displaystyle\Psi_{\gamma}(u,p)=(\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u,\diverge u,u_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}},S(p,u)e_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}})

(3.27)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The case
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
follows from Theorem 3.4 of
[
18
]
. We note that by Remark
3.2
, the calculations performed in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
in
[
18
]
are also valid over the low frequency regime in
\hat{\Gamma}
, therefore the arguments therein can be adapted with minimal modification to handle the cases when
\Sigma\neq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
.
∎

3.3.
Linear analysis with
\eta
and
\kappa

In this section we would like to establish the solvability of the
\gamma
-Stokes system with gravity capillary boundary conditions

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)=f&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge u=g,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
u_{n}+\gamma\partial_{1}\eta=h,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
S(p,u)e_{n}+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n}=k,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0},\end{cases}

(3.28)

for data tuples belonging to the space
\mathcal{Y}^{s}
. First, we introduce the container space for the solution tuple
(u,p,\eta)
.

Definition 3.10
.

For
s\geqslant 0
, we define the Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{s}=\begin{cases}\{(u,p,\eta)\in{}_{0}H^{s+2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\},&R_{\Sigma}\neq\varnothing\\
\{(u,p,\eta)\in{}_{0}H^{s+2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times\mathring{X}^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\},&R_{\Sigma}=\varnothing,\end{cases}

(3.29)

where

\displaystyle\mathring{X}^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})=\{\eta\in X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\mid\hat{\eta}(0)=0\}.

(3.30)

We endow
\mathcal{X}^{s}
with the natural product norm defined via

\displaystyle\left\|(u,p,\eta)\right\|_{\mathcal{X}^{s}}^{2}=\left\|u\right\|_{{}_{0}H^{s+1}}^{2}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{s+1}}^{2}+\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+5/2}}^{2}.

(3.31)

Next, we record an embedding result for
\mathcal{X}^{s}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Suppose
s\geqslant 0
and
\mathcal{X}^{s}
is the Banach space in Definition
3.10
. If
s>n/2
, then we have the continuous inclusion

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{s}\subseteq C_{b}^{\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times C_{b}^{1+\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times C_{0}^{3+\lfloor s-n/2\rfloor}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}).

(3.32)

Moreover, if
(u,p,\eta)\in\mathcal{X}^{s}
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{|x_{R}^{\prime}|\to\infty}\partial^{\alpha}u(x)

\displaystyle=0\;\text{for all}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\;\text{such that}\;|\alpha|\leqslant 2+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor

(3.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{|x_{R}^{\prime}|\to\infty}\partial^{\alpha}p(x)

\displaystyle=0\;\text{for all}\;\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\;\text{such that}\;|\alpha|\leqslant 1+\left\lfloor s-\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor.

(3.34)

Proof.

This follows from standard Sobolev embedding and the first item of Theorem
2.6
.
∎

In the subsection to follow, we establish some preliminary results to be utilized in the subsequent analysis.

3.4.
Preliminary results

In this subsection we use the asymptotics recorded in Section
3.1
to show that we can construct the free surface function
\eta
from a given data tuple
(f,g,h,k)
. First, we study an auxiliary function defined in terms of the multipliers
V,Q
defined in (
3.9
).

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
s\geqslant 0
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
, where
\mathcal{Y}^{s}
is the Hilbert space defined in (
3.23
). Consider
V(\cdot,\cdot,-\gamma):\hat{\Sigma}\times[0,b]\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
Q(\cdot,\cdot,-\gamma):\hat{\Sigma}\times[0,b]\to\mathbb{C}
defined in (
3.9
). We define the measurable function
\psi:\hat{\Sigma}\to\mathbb{C}
via

\displaystyle\psi(\xi)=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\right)\;dx_{n}-\hat{k}(\xi)\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\hat{h}(\xi).

(3.35)

Then the following hold.

(1)

If
R_{\Sigma}=\varnothing
, then
\psi(0)=0
.

(2)

\overline{\psi(\xi)}=\psi(-\xi)
for every
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
.

(3)

We have the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+3/2}|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)^{c}}\;d\xi\lesssim_{n,s,\gamma,b}\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}^{2}.

(3.36)

Proof.

To prove the first item, we assume
R_{\Sigma}=\varnothing
and rewrite

\psi(\xi)=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})(\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-1)\right)\;dx_{n}\\
-\hat{k}(\xi)\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\left(\hat{h}(\xi)-\int_{0}^{b}\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right).

(3.37)

We note that since
h,g
must satisfy (
3.22
), by Remark
3.2
we have
\hat{h}(0)-\int_{0}^{b}\hat{g}(0,x_{n})\;dx_{n}=0
. We also note that by the first item of Theorem
3.5
,
\overline{V(0,x_{n},-\gamma)}=0
and
\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}=0
. The first item follows immediately from these two observations.

To prove the second item, we first note that by using the second item of Theorem
3.5
and by using the fact that
f,g,k,h
are real-valued,

\overline{\psi(\xi)}=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\overline{\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})}\cdot V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)-\overline{\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})}Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)\right)\;dx_{n}-\overline{\hat{k}(\xi)}\cdot V(\xi,b,-\gamma)+\overline{\hat{h}(\xi)}\\
=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\hat{f}(-\xi,x_{n})\cdot\overline{V(-\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(-\xi,x_{n})\overline{Q(-\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\right)\;dx_{n}-\hat{k}(-\xi)\cdot\overline{V(-\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\hat{h}(-\xi)=\psi(-\xi).

(3.38)

To prove the third, we first rewrite
\psi
again as in (
3.37
) and apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to obtain

|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\lesssim\left(\int_{0}^{b}|\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi\right)\left(\int_{0}^{b}|V(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi\right)+\left(\int_{0}^{b}|\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi\right)\left(\int_{0}^{b}|Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)-1|^{2}\;d\xi\right)\\
+|\hat{k}(\xi)|^{2}|V(\xi,b,-\gamma)|^{2}+|\hat{h}(\xi)-\int_{0}^{b}\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\;dx_{n}|^{2}.

(3.39)

We note that by the definition of the
\dot{H}^{-1}
seminorm, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}|\hat{h}(\xi)-\int_{0}^{b}\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\;dx_{n}|^{2}\;d\xi\leqslant\left[h-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}.

(3.40)

Combining (
3.39
), (
3.40
) with (
3.17
), Tonelli’s theorem, and Parseval’s theorem then gives us

\int_{B(0,r)}\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\lesssim_{n,\gamma,b}\int_{B(0,r)}\int_{0}^{b}\left(|\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}+|\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\right)dx_{n}d\xi\\
+\int_{B(0,r)}|\hat{k}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi+\left[h-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}^{2}\lesssim_{n,\gamma,b}\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}^{2}.

(3.41)

If
|\xi|\geqslant 1
, then we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality directly to (
3.35
) and obtain

|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\lesssim\left(\int_{0}^{b}|\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi\right)\left(\int_{0}^{b}|V(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi\right)+\left(\int_{0}^{b}|\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi\right)\left(\int_{0}^{b}|Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)|^{2}\;d\xi\right)\\
+|\hat{k}(\xi)|^{2}|V(\xi,b,-\gamma)|^{2}+|\hat{h}(\xi)|^{2}.

(3.42)

Combining (
3.42
) with (
3.18
), Tonelli’s theorem, and Parseval’s theorem then gives us

\int_{B(0,1)^{c}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+3/2}|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\lesssim_{n,\gamma,b}\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s}|\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi dx_{n}+\int_{0}^{b}\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s}|\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})|^{2}\;d\xi dx_{n}\\
+\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+1/2}|\hat{k}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi+\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+3/2}|\hat{h}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\lesssim_{n,\gamma,b}\int_{0}^{b}\left\|f(\cdot,x_{n})\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\Sigma)}\;dx_{n}\\
+\int_{0}^{b}\left\|g(\cdot,x_{n})\right\|^{2}_{H^{s}(\Sigma)}\;dx_{n}+\left\|k\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{H^{s+3/2}}^{2}\lesssim_{n,s,\gamma,b}\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}^{2}.

(3.43)

∎

Next we study the linear map
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}:\mathcal{X}^{s}\to\mathcal{Y}^{s}
defined via

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)=(\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0),\diverge u,u_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,S(p,u)e_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n})

(3.44)

which is the solution operator corresponding to the system (
3.28
). The next result shows that this linear map is well-defined, bounded, and also injective.

Proposition 3.13
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
\sigma\geqslant 0
, and
s\geqslant 0
. Then the linear map
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}:\mathcal{X}^{s}\to\mathcal{Y}^{s}
defined in (
3.44
) is well-defined, continuous, and injective.

Proof.

We first check that the map is well-defined and continuous. By the fifth item of Theorem
2.5
, the first component
f=\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)
belongs to
H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{{}_{0}H^{s+2}}+\left\|p\right\|_{H^{s+1}}+\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+5/2}}.

(3.45)

The second component
g=\diverge u
belongs to
H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
with the estimate
\left\|g\right\|_{H^{s+1}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{{}_{0}H^{s+2}}
. By the fifth item of Theorem
2.5
and standard trace theory, we deduce that the third component
k=S(p,u)e_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n}
belongs to
H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R}^{n})
with the estimate

\left\|k\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}}\leqslant\left\|p\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma)}+\left\|\mathbb{D}u\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma)}+\left\|\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta\right\|_{H^{s+1/2}(\Sigma)}\lesssim\left\|p\right\|_{H^{s+1}(\Omega)}+\left\|u\right\|_{{}_{0}H^{s+2}}+\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+5/2}}.

(3.46)

By the fifth item of Theorem
2.5
and standard trace theory, the fourth component
h=u_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\gamma\partial_{1}\eta
belongs to
H^{s+3/2}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R})
with the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|h\right\|_{H^{s+3/2}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{{}_{0}H^{s+2}}+\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+5/2}}.

(3.47)

Next we note that since
g=\diverge u
,

\displaystyle u_{n}(x^{\prime},b)-\int_{0}^{b}g(x^{\prime},x_{n})\;dx_{n}=\int_{0}^{b}\partial_{n}u_{n}(x^{\prime},x_{n})\;dx_{n}-\int_{0}^{b}g(x^{\prime},x_{n})=-\diverge^{\prime}\int_{0}^{b}u^{\prime}(x^{\prime},x_{n})\;dx_{n}

(3.48)

for a.e.
x^{\prime}\in\Sigma
. Writing
H^{s+3}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})\ni R(x^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{b}u^{\prime}(x^{\prime},x_{n})\;dx_{n}
, we first note that if
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
, then
\widehat{\diverge R}(0)=0
and
\widehat{\gamma\partial_{1}\eta}(0)=0
. Then we note that

\displaystyle\left[u_{n}-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}=\left[\diverge R\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{L^{2}},

(3.49)

therefore

\displaystyle\left[h-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\leqslant\left[u_{n}-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\;dx_{n}\right]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}+[\gamma\partial_{1}\eta]_{\dot{H}^{-1}}\lesssim\left\|u\right\|_{{}_{0}H^{s+2}}+\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+5/2}}.

(3.50)

Combining the estimates above shows that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is well-defined and continuous.

To show that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is injective, we suppose
(u,p,\eta)\in\mathcal{X}^{s}
and
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}\left(u,p,\eta\right)=0
. We note that if
\tilde{p}=p-\eta
, then
\nabla\tilde{p}=\nabla p-(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0)
and
\tilde{p}I=pI-\eta I
. Therefore
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)=0
if and only if
(u,\tilde{p},\eta)
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\diverge S(\tilde{p},u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
\diverge u=0,&\text{in}\;\Omega\\
S(\tilde{p},u)e_{n}=(\eta-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta)e_{n},&\text{on}\;\Omega_{b}\\
u_{n}+\gamma\partial_{1}\eta=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{b}\\
u=0,&\text{on}\;\Sigma_{0}.\end{cases}

(3.51)

We note that by Tonelli’s theorem, Parseval’s theorem, and the fifth item of Theorem
2.5
we have
\hat{u}(\xi,\cdot)\in H^{s}((0,b);\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
\widehat{\tilde{p}}(\xi,\cdot)\in H^{1}((0,b);\mathbb{C})
, for a.e.
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
. By the second item in Theorem
2.6
,
\hat{\eta}\in L^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})+L^{2}(\Sigma,(1+|\xi|^{2})^{(s+5/2)/2}d\xi;\mathbb{R})
. Thus, we may apply the horizontal Fourier transform to (
3.51
) to deduce for a.e.
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
,
w=\hat{u}(\xi,\cdot),q=\widehat{\tilde{p}}(\xi,\cdot)
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(-\partial_{n}^{2}+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\right)w^{\prime}+2\pi i\xi q-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma w^{\prime}=0,&\text{in}\;(0,b)\\
\left(-\partial_{n}^{2}+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\right)w_{n}+\partial_{n}q-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma w_{n}=0,&\text{in}\;(0,b)\\
2\pi i\xi\cdot w^{\prime}+\partial_{n}w_{n}=0,&\text{in}\;(0,b)\\
-\partial_{n}w^{\prime}-2\pi i\xi w_{n}=0,&\text{for}\;x_{n}=b\\
q-2\partial_{n}w_{n}=(1+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\sigma)\hat{\eta},&\text{for}\;x_{n}=b\\
w_{n}+2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma\hat{\eta}=0,&\text{for}\;x_{n}=b\\
w=0,&\text{for}\;x_{n}=0.\end{cases}

(3.52)

First consider the special case when
R_{\Sigma}=\varnothing
and
\xi=0
. In this case, the third and sixth equations imply that
w_{n}\equiv 0
. Since
\eta\in\mathring{X}^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, we have
\hat{\eta}(0)=0
and therefore by the second and fifth equations,
q\equiv 0
. The first, fourth, and last equations then tell us that
w^{\prime}\equiv 0
. Therefore, we can conclude that at
\xi=0
,
w,q\equiv 0
.

Next consider the general case for which
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}\setminus\{0\}
and (
3.52
) holds. Using (
3.52
) and performing integration by parts (for further details, see Proposition 4.1 in
[
18
]
.), we deduce that

\int_{0}^{b}-\gamma 2\pi i\xi_{1}|w|^{2}+2|\partial_{n}w_{n}|^{2}+|\partial_{n}w^{\prime}+2\pi i\xi w_{n}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|2\pi i\xi\otimes w^{\prime}+w^{\prime}\otimes 2\pi i\xi|^{2}\;dx_{n}=-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma(1+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\sigma)|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}.

(3.53)

By taking the real part of this expression, we see that we must have for a.e.
\xi\in\hat{\Sigma}
,
\partial_{n}w_{n}\equiv 0
and
\partial_{n}w^{\prime}+2\pi i\xi w_{n}\equiv 0
in
(0,b)
, but since
w(0)=0
, we must have
w\equiv 0
in
[0,b]
. Then by the first equation, we must have
q\equiv 0
. By the second to last equation, we find that
\eta\equiv 0
. From this we find that
(u,p,\eta)=(0,0,0)
, so we can conclude that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is injective.

∎

Next we show that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is surjective. To do so we must construct the free surface function
\eta
from a given data tuple
(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
. For the reader’s convenience, we record this construction in the next subsection.

3.5.
Construction of the free surface function and the isomorphism associated to (
3.28
)

Lemma 3.14

(Construction of
\eta
in the presence of surface tension)
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
\sigma>0
and
n\geqslant 2
,
s\geqslant 0
. Then for every
(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
, there exists an
\eta\in X^{s+\frac{5}{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
for which the modified data tuple

\displaystyle(f-(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0),g,h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,k-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n})\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R}^{n})

(3.54)

belongs to the range of
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
defined in (
3.44
). Moreover, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+\frac{5}{2}}}\lesssim\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}.

(3.55)

Proof.

Given
(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
, we propose to define
\eta\in X^{s+\frac{5}{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
through

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}(\xi)=\begin{cases}\frac{\psi(\xi)}{\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)},&\xi\neq 0\\
0,&\xi=0,\end{cases}

(3.56)

where
\rho_{\gamma}
is defined in (
3.19
) and
\psi
is defined in terms of
(f,g,h,k)
in (
3.35
). We note that the choice of
\hat{\eta}(0)=0
is only relevant in the case when
R_{\Gamma}=\varnothing
.

We first note that by the first item of Lemma
3.6
and the second item of Lemma
3.12
, we have
\overline{\hat{\eta}(\xi)}=\hat{\eta}(-\xi)
. Furthermore, by the second item of Lemma
3.6
and the third item of Lemma
3.12
, we have the estimate

\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{\xi_{1}^{2}+|\xi|^{4}}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+\frac{5}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)^{c}}\right)|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\\
\lesssim\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+\frac{3}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)^{c}}\right)|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\\
\lesssim\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+\frac{3}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,r)^{c}}\right)|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\lesssim\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}^{2}.

(3.57)

Consequently, if we define
\eta=(\hat{\eta})^{\vee}
, then by (
3.57
), and
\overline{\hat{\eta}(\xi)}=\hat{\eta}(\xi)
, we have
\eta\in X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
with the estimate (
3.55
).

To conclude our proof it suffices to show that the modified data given in (
3.54
) belongs to the range of
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)
. By Theorem
3.9
, it suffices to show that the modified data tuple has the desired regularity and satisfies the divergence-trace compatibility condition (
3.22
) and the adjoint compatibility condition (
3.25
). We note that since
\eta\in X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
, by the fifth item of Theorem
2.5
and the third item of Theorem
2.6
, we have
(f,g,h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,k-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n})\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
(h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta)-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{n})\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
. To check (
3.25
), we write
0=\psi-\rho_{\gamma}\hat{\eta}
for
\xi\neq 0
and use the definition of
\psi
and
\rho_{\gamma}
to obtain

0=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\right)\;dx_{n}\\
-(\hat{k}(\xi)+(1+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\sigma)\hat{\eta}(\xi)e_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\hat{h}(\xi)-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma\hat{\eta}(\xi).

(3.58)

Using the third equation in (
3.10
) we have
2\pi i\xi\cdot V^{\prime}(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)+\partial_{n}V_{n}(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)=0
, and since
V(\xi,0,-\gamma)=0
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{V_{n}(\xi,b,-\gamma)}=\int_{0}^{b}\partial_{n}\overline{V_{n}(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\;dx_{n}=\int_{0}^{b}2\pi i\xi\cdot\overline{V^{\prime}(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\;dx_{n}.

(3.59)

Thus, we can rewrite (
3.58
) as

0=\int_{0}^{b}\left(\left(\hat{f}(\xi,x_{n})-(\widehat{\nabla^{\prime}\eta}(\xi),0)\right)\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{n})\overline{Q(\xi,x_{n},-\gamma)}\right)\;dx_{n}\\
-(\hat{k}(\xi)+\widehat{\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta}(\xi)e_{n})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}+\hat{h}(\xi)-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma\hat{\eta}(\xi),

(3.60)

and the desired conclusion follows immediately.
∎

For the special case of
n=2
, we can also construct the free surface function
\eta
in the case without surface tension.

Lemma 3.15

(Construction of the free surface function without surface tension)
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
\sigma=0
and
n=2
,
s\geqslant 0
. Then for every
(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
, there exists an
\eta\in H^{s+\frac{5}{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
for which the modified data tuple

\displaystyle(f-\partial_{1}\eta e_{1},g,h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,k)\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R}^{n})

(3.61)

belongs to the range of
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
defined in (
3.44
). Moreover, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\eta\right\|_{X^{s+\frac{5}{2}}}\lesssim\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}.

(3.62)

Proof.

We follow the construction in the previous lemma and propose to define
\eta
via (
3.56
). Then
\overline{\hat{\eta}(\xi)}=\hat{\eta}(-\xi)
,
\hat{\eta}(0)=0
, and by the third item of Lemma
3.6
and the third item of Lemma
3.12
, we have

\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\left((1+|\xi|^{2})\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+\frac{5}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}\right)|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\\
\lesssim\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+\frac{3}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}\right)|\rho_{\gamma}(\xi)|^{2}|\hat{\eta}(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\\
\lesssim\int_{\hat{\Sigma}}\left(\frac{1}{|\xi|^{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)}+(1+|\xi|^{2})^{s+\frac{3}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{B(0,1)^{c}}\right)|\psi(\xi)|^{2}\;d\xi\lesssim\left\|(f,g,h,k)\right\|_{\mathcal{Y}^{s}}^{2}.

(3.63)

Consequently, we may define
\eta=(\hat{\eta})^{\vee}
. By (
3.63
) and Lemma
2.2
, we have
\eta\in H^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})=X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
with the estimate (
3.62
). By the third item of Theorem
2.6
, we have
(f-\partial_{1}\eta e_{1},g,h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,k)\in H^{s}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{3}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R})\times H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\Sigma_{b};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta)-\int_{0}^{b}g(\cdot,x_{2})\;dx_{2}\in\dot{H}^{-1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
.
To conclude we follow the same line of calculations as in Lemma
3.14
to arrive at

0=\int_{0}^{b}\left((\hat{f}(\xi,x_{2})-2\pi i\xi_{1}\hat{\eta}e_{1})\cdot\overline{V(\xi,x_{2},-\gamma)}-\hat{g}(\xi,x_{2})\overline{Q(\xi,x_{2},-\gamma)}\right)\;dx_{2}-\hat{k}(\xi)\cdot\overline{V(\xi,b,-\gamma)}\\
+\hat{h}(\xi)-2\pi i\xi_{1}\gamma\hat{\eta}(\xi),

(3.64)

which verifies the overdetermined compatibility condition (
3.25
).
∎

Now we are ready to prove that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}:\mathcal{X}^{s}\to\mathcal{Y}^{s}
is an isomorphism when
\sigma>0
and
n\geqslant 2
, and when
\sigma=0
and
n=2
.

Theorem 3.16

(Existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
3.28
))
.

Suppose
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},s\geqslant 0
.

(1)

(Isomorphism in the case with surface tension) If
\sigma>0
and
n\geqslant 2
, then the bounded linear map
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}:\mathcal{X}^{s}\to\mathcal{Y}^{s}
defined in (
3.44
) is an isomorphism.

(2)

(Isomorphism in the case without surface tension) If
\sigma=0
and
n=2
, then the bounded linear map
\Upsilon_{\gamma,0}:\mathcal{X}^{s}\to\mathcal{Y}^{s}
defined in (
3.44
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

To prove the first item, by Proposition
3.13
, it suffices to show that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is surjective. Suppose
(f,g,h,k)\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
, and define the free surface function
\eta\in X^{s+5/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
by the construction in Lemma
3.14
. By Theorem
3.9
, there exists
(u,p)\in{}_{0}H^{s+2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})\times H^{s+1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
such that
\Psi_{\gamma}(u,p)=(\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u,\diverge u,u_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}},S(p,u)e_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}})=(f-(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0),g,h-\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,k-\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n})
. Therefore, we find that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)=(f,g,h,k)
. This shows that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is surjective, and it follows that
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
is an isomorphism.

To prove the second item we follow the same argument as above, using Lemma
3.15
in place of Lemma
3.14
and
\Upsilon_{\gamma,0}
in place of
\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}
.
∎

3.6.
Parameter regime for the linear isomorphism associated to (
1.24
)

In this subsection we consider the linearization of the flattened system (
1.24
) around the trivial solution
u=0,p=0,\eta=0
, which is given by the map
\mathcal{X}^{s}\ni(u,p,\eta)\mapsto L_{\kappa,\sigma}\in\mathcal{Y}^{s}
defined in (
1.42
). Our goal is to show that there is a parameter regime in terms of
\kappa
for which
L_{\kappa,\sigma}
remains an isomorphism between
\mathcal{X}^{s}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{s}
. To achieve this we first define
L_{0,\sigma},M_{\kappa}:\mathcal{X}^{s}\to\mathcal{Y}^{s}
via

L_{0,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)=\Upsilon_{\gamma,\sigma}(u,p,\eta)=(\diverge S(p,u)-\gamma\partial_{1}u+(\nabla^{\prime}\eta,0),\diverge u,u_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\gamma\partial_{1}\eta,S(p,u)e_{n}\rvert_{\Sigma_{b}}+\sigma\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{n}),

(3.65)

and

M_{\kappa}(u,p,\eta)=(-\gamma\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}+\kappa s_{0}(x_{n})\partial_{1}u+\kappa s_{0}^{\prime}(x_{n})u_{n}e_{1}+\kappa^{2}x_{n}s_{0}(x_{n})\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}-\kappa x_{n}\Delta^{\prime}\eta e_{1}-\kappa(x_{n}\nabla^{\prime}\partial_{1}\eta,\partial_{1}\eta),\\
\kappa x_{n}\partial_{1}\eta,-\frac{\kappa b^{2}}{2}\partial_{1}\eta,0),

(3.66)

so that
L_{\kappa,\sigma}=L_{0,\sigma}+M_{\kappa}=L_{0,\sigma}(I+L_{0,\sigma}^{-1}M_{\kappa})
, where
L_{\kappa,\sigma}
is defined via (
1.42
) and
s_{0}
is defined via (
1.6
). Our goal in this subsection is to show that
\left\|M_{\kappa}\right\|<\frac{1}{\left\|L_{0,\sigma}\right\|^{-1}}
for sufficiently small
\kappa
.

We first establish a preliminary lemma. While the following result is not at all surprising and follows from routine applications of Tonelli’s theorem and Hölder’s inequality, we record the proof below to show that the universal constants in the estimates are purely combinatorial and do not depend on the physical parameters.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}\ni n\geqslant 2
and suppose
f\in C^{\infty}([0,b];\mathbb{R})
. The following hold.

(1)

If
g\in H^{k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some integer
k\geqslant 0
and for any
i\in\mathbb{N},j\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
|j|\leqslant k
we define
(\partial^{i}f\partial^{j}g)(x)=\partial^{i}f(x_{n})\partial^{j}g(x)
, then we have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial^{i}f\partial^{j}g\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|\partial^{i}f\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{j}}.

(3.67)

Consequently,
fg\in H^{k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and there exists a combinatorial constant
C=C(k)>0
such that

\displaystyle\left\|fg\right\|_{H^{k}}\leqslant C\max_{0\leqslant i\leqslant k}\left\|\partial^{i}f\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{k}}.

(3.68)

(2)

If
g\in H^{k}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
for some integer
k\geqslant 0
and for any
i\in\mathbb{N},j\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}
with
|j|\leqslant k
we define
(\partial^{i}f\partial^{j}g)(x)=\partial^{i}f(x_{n})\partial^{j}g(x^{\prime})
, then we have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial^{i}f\partial^{j}g\right\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\left\|\partial^{i}f\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|g\right\|_{H^{j}}.

(3.69)

Consequently,
fg\in H^{k}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
